**Start with a skeleton, not the models.** In Week 1, get one thin end-to-end path working (mic → 2–3 s chunk → dummy score → React gauge), while each person starts their own stream. Integration is where student projects die, so prove the pipe early.

**Day 1–2: shared setup (all 3 together)**
1. One GitHub monorepo: `/audio_spoof`, `/intent_nlp`, `/voiceprint`, `/fusion`, `/genai`, `/backend`, `/frontend`.
2. Freeze the interface now. Each stream takes a 16 kHz mono chunk (or text chunk) and returns a JSON like `{stream, score, evidence, timestamp}`. Fusion and the frontend depend on this.
3. FastAPI + WebSocket stub that returns random scores to your existing React dashboard.

**Week 1–2: three parallel workstreams**

*A. Audio spoof stream*
- Download ASVspoof 2019 LA plus In-the-Wild.
- Extract frozen wav2vec2 features, then train a small classifier head (e.g., attention pooling + MLP) in PyTorch.
- Get a baseline EER first. Improve it later.

*B. NLP intent stream + data*
- Collect the Kaggle Fraud-Call-India seed set and hand-label about 100–200 transcripts.
- Use an LLM to generate Hindi/Hinglish scam and normal transcripts, labelled by category (urgency, OTP request, impersonation, payment pressure), and spot-check them.
- Run Whisper on a few test clips, then fine-tune a MuRIL/HingBERT head.

*C. Voiceprint + fusion + GenAI*
- ECAPA-TDNN (SpeechBrain): enroll 3–4 voices, compute cosine similarity, and find a threshold.
- Build the fusion data generator. You need *simulated streaming sequences* of the three scores to train the Temporal Risk Fusion Network, so write that early.
- Prompt-draft the challenge-response and explainer agents.

**Week 3 onward:** follow your roadmap. Integrate at the end of Week 4–5, run evaluation, and rehearse the demo.

**Three things to fix before you start**
- **Scope for 3 people.** The synopsis assumes 4–5. Drop HDBSCAN campaign clustering to "stretch goal" so it doesn't sink you.
- **Timeline.** Your roadmap is 12 weeks, but you have roughly 10 left. Set internal deadlines: all streams working standalone by about Week 4, integrated by Week 6.
- **Viva inconsistency.** Section 5 says the heads are "built from scratch", but the team table says "fine-tuned RawNet2/AASIST". Pick one story. The custom head on frozen features is the defensible one.

Want me to turn Week 1 into a day-by-day checklist for each of the three of you, or start with the exact code skeleton for the stream interface and WebSocket stub?

Solo changes the plan: you can't build everything in parallel, so build **in dependency order** and protect a working demo at every stage. You have about 10 weeks left.

## Priority tiers (decide this now)
- **Must have (the viva core):** spoof stream, intent stream, fusion network, live demo
- **Should have:** voiceprint stream, evidence explainer, challenge-response agent
- **Stretch:** campaign clustering (HDBSCAN), user studies, adversarial test

## Day 1–3: Setup
1. Create a GitHub repo with folders `audio/`, `nlp/`, `voice/`, `fusion/`, `genai/`, `backend/`, `frontend/`.
2. Use **Kaggle or Colab free GPU** for training. Do inference on your laptop.
3. Set up Python with PyTorch, `transformers`, `speechbrain`, `librosa`, `openai-whisper`, `fastapi`.
4. Define one output format for every stream: `{stream, score(0–1), evidence, t}`. Fusion and the frontend both depend on it.

## Week 1–2: Spoof detection stream (do this first)
1. Download ASVspoof 2019 LA and In-the-Wild.
2. Run frozen wav2vec2 over the audio once and **save the features to disk**. This saves hours of repeated compute.
3. Train a small head on the saved features (attention pooling + MLP) with PyTorch.
4. Measure EER, FAR, FRR and ROC-AUC. Get a baseline number before improving anything.

## Week 3–4: Intent NLP stream and data
1. Take the Kaggle Fraud-Call-India seed set and hand-label 100–200 samples.
2. Prompt an LLM to generate Hindi/Hinglish scam and normal transcripts, labelled across your 4 categories. Spot-check about 10% and mark them as synthetic.
3. Test Whisper small on a few Hindi/Hinglish clips.
4. Train a head on frozen MuRIL/HingBERT embeddings. Report per-category F1.

## Week 5: Voiceprint stream
Use SpeechBrain ECAPA-TDNN. Enroll 3–4 voices (friends/family, with consent), compute cosine similarity, and pick a threshold. Report EER.

## Week 6–7: Fusion and backend
1. Generate **simulated streaming sequences** of the three scores (scam call vs genuine call) to train on, plus real ones from your streams.
2. Build the Temporal Risk Fusion Network (small Transformer or GRU with attention) and a static-MLP baseline for the ablation.
3. Build the FastAPI + WebSocket server with a 2–3 s chunk buffer that discards audio after scoring.
4. Connect your existing React dashboard to it.

## Week 8: GenAI agents
Write the challenge-response and evidence-explainer prompts. Both take the fusion evidence trail as input.

## Week 9–10: Evaluation, demo, report
Run the accent/language split, the latency check, the live-call demo on speakerphone, and the report.

## Risks to watch
- **Biggest risk:** Hindi/Hinglish data quality. Start the data work in Week 1 alongside the spoof stream.
- **Latency:** Whisper plus wav2vec2 on a laptop CPU may be slow. Test early and use the smaller models.
- **Scope:** if you're behind at the end of Week 6, drop the stretch items and keep Must plus Should.

Do you want to begin with the exact commands and starter code for the spoof stream (Week 1)?

## Phase 1: Setup and the spoof detection stream

### Step 1: Project setup (Day 1)
```bash
mkdir rakshavoice && cd rakshavoice
git init
mkdir audio nlp voice fusion genai backend frontend data
python -m venv venv && source venv/bin/activate   # Windows: venv\Scripts\activate
pip install torch torchaudio transformers librosa soundfile numpy scikit-learn tqdm
```
Push the repo to GitHub. Commit after every working step.

### Step 2: Get the data (Day 1–2)
- **ASVspoof 2019 LA**: search "ASVspoof 2019 LA" on Kaggle or the Edinburgh DataShare. You need the `train`, `dev` and `eval` audio plus the protocol text files.
- **In-the-Wild**: search "In-the-Wild audio deepfake dataset". Use it later for generalization testing, not for training.
- Start on **Kaggle notebooks** (free GPU, and the dataset is already there if you add it).

Each protocol line ends in `bonafide` or `spoof`. That is your label.

### Step 3: Extract and cache features (Day 2–4)
Run wav2vec2 once, then save the features. Never rerun it during training.

```python
# audio/extract.py
import torch, librosa, numpy as np
from transformers import Wav2Vec2Model, Wav2Vec2FeatureExtractor
dev = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
fe = Wav2Vec2FeatureExtractor.from_pretrained("facebook/wav2vec2-base")
m = Wav2Vec2Model.from_pretrained("facebook/wav2vec2-base").to(dev).eval()
SR, LEN = 16000, 64000   # 4 s clips

def feats(path):
    y, _ = librosa.load(path, sr=SR)
    y = np.pad(y, (0, max(0, LEN-len(y))))[:LEN]
    x = fe(y, sampling_rate=SR, return_tensors="pt").input_values.to(dev)
    with torch.no_grad():
        hs = m(x, output_hidden_states=True).hidden_states   # 13 layers
    return torch.stack([h.mean(1).squeeze(0) for h in hs]).half().cpu().numpy()  # (13,768)
```
Loop over the protocol file, build `X` of shape `(N, 13, 768)` and labels `y` (bonafide=0, spoof=1), then `np.save` both for train, dev and eval. **Test on 500 files first.** Full extraction takes a while.

### Step 4: Train the classifier head (Day 5–8)
This is the part you build "from scratch":
```python
# audio/head.py
import torch, torch.nn as nn
class SpoofHead(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, L=13, D=768):
        super().__init__()
        self.w = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(L))          # learned layer weights
        self.net = nn.Sequential(nn.LayerNorm(D), nn.Linear(D,256),
                                 nn.GELU(), nn.Dropout(0.3), nn.Linear(256,1))
    def forward(self, x):                               # x: (B,13,768)
        a = torch.softmax(self.w, 0)[None,:,None]
        return self.net((x*a).sum(1)).squeeze(-1)
```
Train with `BCEWithLogitsLoss`, Adam at 1e-3 and about 20 epochs. Use dev for early stopping.

### Step 5: Evaluate (Day 9–10)
```python
from sklearn.metrics import roc_curve, roc_auc_score
def eer(y, s):
    fpr, tpr, _ = roc_curve(y, s); fnr = 1 - tpr
    i = np.nanargmin(np.abs(fnr - fpr)); return (fpr[i]+fnr[i])/2
```
Report EER and ROC-AUC on **eval**. Then test on a small In-the-Wild sample. Expect a big drop there, and that is a good result for your report because it supports your "generalization" evaluation.

### Phase 1 is done when
- `X_train.npy`, `X_dev.npy`, `X_eval.npy` exist
- Your head reaches an EER number you can write down
- A function `score_chunk(wav) -> float` returns a 0–1 score for a 4 s chunk (this is what the backend will call later)

### Watch out for
- Don't train on eval. Keep it for the final numbers only.
- If Kaggle runs out of memory, extract in batches and save every 1000 files.

Tell me when the extraction runs, or paste any error, and we'll fix it before moving to Phase 2.

[Thinking (summary)]
Explaining phase one steps in plain, beginner-friendly detail.
[End of Thinking (summary)]

Here is each step explained: what it is, why you do it, and exactly how.

## Step 1: Project setup

**What it is:** a clean folder and an isolated Python environment, so libraries don't clash with other projects.

1. Install Python 3.10 or 3.11 and Git, then create a free account on GitHub and Kaggle.
2. Run these in a terminal:
```bash
mkdir rakshavoice && cd rakshavoice
git init
python -m venv venv
venv\Scripts\activate          # Windows (Mac/Linux: source venv/bin/activate)
pip install torch torchaudio transformers librosa soundfile numpy scikit-learn tqdm
mkdir audio nlp voice fusion genai backend frontend data
```
3. On GitHub, create an empty repo, then run `git remote add origin <url>`, `git add .`, `git commit -m "init"`, `git push -u origin main`.

You will do the heavy training on Kaggle, not your laptop. Your laptop is only for editing code and later for the live demo.

## Step 2: Get the data

**What the dataset is:** ASVspoof 2019 LA contains thousands of short speech clips. Some are real human speech (**bonafide**), others are machine-generated or converted (**spoof**). Your model learns to tell them apart.

**How to get it on Kaggle:**
1. Go to kaggle.com, click **Create → New Notebook**.
2. In the right sidebar, click **Add Input** and search "ASVspoof 2019 LA". Pick a dataset that has the `ASVspoof2019_LA_train`, `_dev`, `_eval` folders and a `ASVspoof2019_LA_cm_protocols` folder.
3. In the right sidebar under **Session options**, set **Accelerator** to GPU (T4 or P100).
4. Run `!ls /kaggle/input` to see the exact folder name. Dataset names differ, so adjust the paths below to match.

**The protocol file** is a text file where each line looks like this:
```
LA_0079 LA_T_1138215 - - bonafide
```
The 2nd column is the file name, and the last column is the label. You will read this file to know which audio is real or fake.

## Step 3: Extract and cache features

**What wav2vec2 is, in plain English:** a pretrained model that "listens" to audio and turns it into numbers describing what it hears. It was trained by Facebook on huge amounts of speech. You do not train it. You only use it as a feature extractor, exactly as your synopsis says ("frozen").

**Why cache:** running wav2vec2 on 25,000 clips takes long. If you ran it again every training epoch, 20 epochs would cost 20 times the time. So run it once, save the results to a file, and train on the file.

**What the numbers look like:** wav2vec2-base has 12 layers plus an input layer, so 13 layers. Each layer outputs a 768-number vector per time step. Averaging over time gives one `(13, 768)` array per clip. Different layers capture different things, and the spoof artifacts often hide in the middle layers. That is why you keep all 13 and let your head learn which ones matter.

**Full extraction code (batched, so it's fast):**
```python
import torch, librosa, numpy as np
from tqdm import tqdm
from transformers import Wav2Vec2Model, Wav2Vec2FeatureExtractor

dev = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
fe = Wav2Vec2FeatureExtractor.from_pretrained("facebook/wav2vec2-base")
model = Wav2Vec2Model.from_pretrained("facebook/wav2vec2-base").to(dev).eval()
SR, LEN = 16000, 64000          # 16 kHz, 4 seconds = 64000 samples

def load(path):
    y, _ = librosa.load(path, sr=SR)           # resample to 16 kHz mono
    if len(y) < LEN:                            # too short: pad with silence
        y = np.pad(y, (0, LEN - len(y)))
    return y[:LEN]                              # too long: cut to 4 s

def read_protocol(proto_path):
    names, labels = [], []
    for line in open(proto_path):
        p = line.split()
        names.append(p[1])
        labels.append(0 if p[-1] == "bonafide" else 1)   # 0=real, 1=spoof
    return names, np.array(labels)

def extract(audio_dir, proto_path, out_prefix, limit=None, bs=32):
    names, labels = read_protocol(proto_path)
    if limit: names, labels = names[:limit], labels[:limit]
    X = np.zeros((len(names), 13, 768), dtype=np.float16)
    for i in tqdm(range(0, len(names), bs)):
        batch = [load(f"{audio_dir}/{n}.flac") for n in names[i:i+bs]]
        x = fe(batch, sampling_rate=SR, return_tensors="pt").input_values.to(dev)
        with torch.no_grad():
            hs = model(x, output_hidden_states=True).hidden_states
        X[i:i+len(batch)] = torch.stack([h.mean(1) for h in hs], 1).half().cpu().numpy()
    np.save(f"{out_prefix}_X.npy", X)
    np.save(f"{out_prefix}_y.npy", labels)
```
**How to run it:** call `extract(...)` with the audio folder (the one containing the `.flac` files) and the matching protocol file, once each for train, dev and eval.

1. First do `limit=500` as a test. If it finishes in about a minute and gives no errors, continue.
2. Then run the full train and dev sets. The eval set is large (about 70,000 clips). If time is tight, use `limit=15000` for eval.
3. Kaggle sessions time out, so after each `extract` finishes, the `.npy` files appear in `/kaggle/working`. Click **Save Version** and download them or keep them as a Kaggle dataset.

## Step 4: Train the classifier head

**What this is:** a small neural network that takes the `(13, 768)` array and outputs one number: how likely the clip is spoofed. This small network is the part you designed and trained, which is your "built from scratch" claim.

**How it works:**
- `self.w` holds 13 learnable weights. The softmax turns them into percentages, and the model takes a weighted sum of the 13 layers. So it learns *which layers matter for detecting fakes*. Mention this in the viva, because it is a genuine design choice.
- Then `LayerNorm → Linear(768→256) → GELU → Dropout → Linear(256→1)` produces the score.
- Dropout (0.3) randomly switches off neurons while training. It stops the network from memorizing the training data.

**Training loop:**
```python
import torch, torch.nn as nn, numpy as np
from torch.utils.data import TensorDataset, DataLoader

class SpoofHead(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, L=13, D=768):
        super().__init__()
        self.w = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(L))
        self.net = nn.Sequential(nn.LayerNorm(D), nn.Linear(D,256),
                                 nn.GELU(), nn.Dropout(0.3), nn.Linear(256,1))
    def forward(self, x):
        a = torch.softmax(self.w, 0)[None, :, None]
        return self.net((x * a).sum(1)).squeeze(-1)

def loader(prefix, shuffle):
    X = torch.tensor(np.load(f"{prefix}_X.npy")).float()
    y = torch.tensor(np.load(f"{prefix}_y.npy")).float()
    return DataLoader(TensorDataset(X, y), batch_size=128, shuffle=shuffle)

tr, dv = loader("train", True), loader("dev", False)
head = SpoofHead().to(dev)
opt = torch.optim.Adam(head.parameters(), lr=1e-3)
loss_fn = nn.BCEWithLogitsLoss()

best = 9
for ep in range(20):
    head.train()
    for x, y in tr:
        x, y = x.to(dev), y.to(dev)
        opt.zero_grad(); loss_fn(head(x), y).backward(); opt.step()
    # validate
    head.eval(); s, t = [], []
    with torch.no_grad():
        for x, y in dv:
            s += torch.sigmoid(head(x.to(dev))).cpu().tolist(); t += y.tolist()
    e = eer(np.array(t), np.array(s))
    print(ep, "dev EER:", e)
    if e < best: best = e; torch.save(head.state_dict(), "spoof_head.pt")
```
The code keeps the checkpoint with the **lowest dev EER**, which is called early stopping by checkpoint.

## Step 5: Evaluate

**What EER is:** the point where the false accept rate equals the false reject rate. Lower is better. 0% would be perfect and 50% is random guessing.

```python
from sklearn.metrics import roc_curve, roc_auc_score
def eer(y, s):
    fpr, tpr, _ = roc_curve(y, s)
    fnr = 1 - tpr
    i = np.nanargmin(np.abs(fnr - fpr))
    return (fpr[i] + fnr[i]) / 2
```
After training, load the best checkpoint, score the **eval** set once, and record EER and `roc_auc_score`. This is the number for your report. Also write down FAR and FRR at that threshold.

For In-the-Wild, extract features the same way (it has its own metadata file with labels) and test the same checkpoint without retraining. Expect a much worse EER. That is normal and is exactly the generalization finding your synopsis promises to report honestly.

## Step 6: Make the reusable scoring function

This is what your backend will call during a live call:
```python
head.load_state_dict(torch.load("spoof_head.pt")); head.eval()

def score_chunk(wav):             # wav: numpy array, 16 kHz, mono
    if len(wav) < LEN: wav = np.pad(wav, (0, LEN - len(wav)))
    x = fe([wav[:LEN]], sampling_rate=SR, return_tensors="pt").input_values.to(dev)
    with torch.no_grad():
        hs = model(x, output_hidden_states=True).hidden_states
        f = torch.stack([h.mean(1) for h in hs], 1).float()
        return torch.sigmoid(head(f)).item()
```
Copy this into `audio/spoof_stream.py`. A score near 1 means "probably synthetic".

## Common problems
- **Out of memory:** lower `bs` to 16.
- **Wrong file path or FileNotFound:** print `names[0]` and check it matches an actual file in the audio folder.
- **EER stuck near 50%:** check that the labels are the right way round and that you saved features in the same order as the labels.
- **Session times out:** save outputs after every split.

## Summary
Phase 1 turns raw audio into a spoof score in five moves: set up the project, get ASVspoof data, run wav2vec2 once and cache the features, train your small custom head on those features, then measure EER on unseen data and wrap the result in `score_chunk()`. Once you have one EER number and a working `score_chunk()`, Phase 1 is complete.

Run the 500-clip test first and tell me what you see, or paste any error you hit.

## Phase 1 entirely on Kaggle

Everything below runs in Kaggle notebook cells. You need nothing installed on your laptop except a browser.

### Step 1: Create the notebook
1. Sign in at kaggle.com. Verify your phone number under **Settings → Phone verification**. GPU and Internet stay locked until you do.
2. Click **Create → New Notebook** and name it `rakshavoice-phase1-spoof`.
3. Right sidebar → **Session options**:
   - **Accelerator:** GPU T4 x2 (or P100)
   - **Internet:** On (needed to download wav2vec2 weights)
4. Click **Add Input** (top right), search **ASVspoof 2019 LA**, and add a dataset that has `train`, `dev`, `eval` audio and the protocol files. Also search **In-the-Wild** and add it (used later in Step 6).

Kaggle already has torch, transformers, librosa and scikit-learn installed, so **no pip install is needed**. Check with this first cell:
```python
import torch, transformers, librosa
print(torch.cuda.is_available(), torch.__version__)   # must print True
```

### Step 2: Find your data paths
Dataset folder names vary, so look at them first:
```python
!ls /kaggle/input
!find /kaggle/input -maxdepth 4 -name "*.txt" | head -20
!find /kaggle/input -maxdepth 4 -type d | head -30
```
Note down three things for each split (train, dev, eval): the audio folder containing the `.flac` files, and the matching protocol `.txt` file. Put them in one cell:
```python
BASE = "/kaggle/input/<your-dataset-name>"   # replace after running the find
P = {
 "train": (f"{BASE}/.../flac", f"{BASE}/.../ASVspoof2019.LA.cm.train.trn.txt"),
 "dev":   (f"{BASE}/.../flac", f"{BASE}/.../ASVspoof2019.LA.cm.dev.trl.txt"),
 "eval":  (f"{BASE}/.../flac", f"{BASE}/.../ASVspoof2019.LA.cm.eval.trl.txt"),
}
```
Verify with `print(open(P["train"][1]).readline())`. You should see a line ending in `bonafide` or `spoof`.

### Step 3: Feature extraction (Cells)
Paste the `load`, `read_protocol` and `extract` code from my previous message into cells, then run the **test first**:
```python
extract(*P["train"], out_prefix="/kaggle/working/test500", limit=500)
```
If it finishes in about 1 minute with no error, run the real ones, **one split per cell**:
```python
extract(*P["train"], out_prefix="/kaggle/working/train")
extract(*P["dev"],   out_prefix="/kaggle/working/dev")
extract(*P["eval"],  out_prefix="/kaggle/working/eval", limit=15000)
```
Why one per cell: if the session dies, you don't lose the finished splits.

Everything saved to `/kaggle/working` is your output (20 GB limit).

### Step 4: Save progress so it survives
Kaggle clears the working folder when a session ends unless you save a version.
1. Click **Save Version → Save & Run All (Commit)**. Outputs are then stored with that version.
2. Better: after extraction is done, open the notebook's **Output** tab and click **New Dataset**. Name it `rakshavoice-spoof-features`. In later sessions, add it as an Input and read from `/kaggle/input/rakshavoice-spoof-features/`, so you never re-extract.

### Step 5: Train the head
In a new notebook (or the same one) paste the `SpoofHead`, `loader`, `eer` code and the training loop. If you're loading features from your saved dataset, change the paths:
```python
tr = loader("/kaggle/input/rakshavoice-spoof-features/train", True)
dv = loader("/kaggle/input/rakshavoice-spoof-features/dev", False)
```
Training on cached features takes only a few minutes. The checkpoint `spoof_head.pt` saves to `/kaggle/working`.

### Step 6: Evaluate
Load the saved head, compute EER and AUC on the eval features. For In-the-Wild, run `extract` on its audio using its metadata file, then score with the same head without retraining.

### Step 7: Save everything
Commit the notebook again and create a second dataset from the output containing `spoof_head.pt` and the results. Also keep the code: download the `.ipynb` and later put it in your GitHub repo (via the notebook's **File → Download**).

### Kaggle limits to remember
- **GPU quota:** about 30 hours a week. Extraction is the heavy part, so don't waste GPU hours on idle sessions. Stop the session when done.
- **Session limit:** around 9–12 hours, and it also stops if idle. Hence save each split separately.
- **Internet is on only for notebooks you enable it on.** Model weights download once per session.

Run Step 1 and the Step 2 `find` commands, then paste the output here and I'll fill in the exact paths for your dataset.

## What Phase 1 should produce

### 1. Files generated

| # | File | Created in | Size (approx.) | Purpose |
|---|------|-----------|------|---------|
| 1 | `train_X.npy`, `train_y.npy` | Step 3 | ~500 MB / ~200 KB | Cached wav2vec2 features and labels for 25,380 training clips |
| 2 | `dev_X.npy`, `dev_y.npy` | Step 3 | ~500 MB / ~200 KB | Validation features (24,844 clips) |
| 3 | `eval_X.npy`, `eval_y.npy` | Step 3 | ~300 MB for 15,000 clips (about 1.4 GB if full) | Final test features |
| 4 | `itw_X.npy`, `itw_y.npy` | Step 6 | depends on the sample | In-the-Wild features for generalization |
| 5 | `spoof_head.pt` | Step 5 | ~1 MB | **Your trained model.** The most important file |
| 6 | `results.json` | Step 6 | tiny | EER, AUC, FAR, FRR, threshold, split sizes |
| 7 | `roc_curve.png` and `training_curve.png` | Step 6 | small | Figures for the report |
| 8 | `spoof_stream.py` | Step 6 | tiny | The `score_chunk(wav)` function for the backend |
| 9 | `rakshavoice-phase1-spoof.ipynb` | Step 7 | small | The full notebook, as proof of work |

Delete `test500_*` after the test run.

### 2. Outputs you should see in the notebook

| Step | What you should see |
|------|---------------------|
| GPU check | `True` and the torch version |
| Protocol check | A line ending in `bonafide` or `spoof` |
| Extraction | A progress bar that finishes with no errors, and `X.shape == (N, 13, 768)` |
| Training | `dev EER` printed each epoch, **falling** over the first epochs, then flattening |
| Evaluation | One final line with eval EER and AUC |
| Scoring function | `score_chunk(wav)` returns a single number between 0 and 1 |

Add these three sanity prints to confirm correctness:
```python
print(X.shape, y.shape, y.mean())     # y.mean() is the spoof fraction (ASVspoof train is ~90% spoof)
print(np.isnan(X).sum())              # must be 0
print(score_chunk(real_wav), score_chunk(fake_wav))   # fake should score higher
```

### 3. Realistic numbers (rough ballpark, not guarantees)

| Dataset | Likely EER | Meaning |
|---------|-----------|---------|
| ASVspoof dev | ~1–8% | Attacks are the same types seen in training |
| ASVspoof eval | ~5–20% | Includes attack types unseen in training, so it is higher |
| In-the-Wild | ~25–45% | Real-world fakes differ a lot, so a big drop is expected |

If dev EER is near 50%, something is wrong (usually labels or feature order). If dev EER is exactly 0%, you probably leaked data or the files are mismatched. If the In-the-Wild EER is bad, that is **not a failure**. It is the honest generalization result your synopsis promises to report.

### 4. What to save and where

1. **Kaggle dataset 1: `rakshavoice-spoof-features`** contains the `.npy` files. This saves hours, because you never re-extract.
2. **Kaggle dataset 2: `rakshavoice-spoof-model`** contains `spoof_head.pt`, `results.json`, the plots and `spoof_stream.py`.
3. **Your laptop:** download the notebook `.ipynb`, `spoof_head.pt`, `spoof_stream.py`, `results.json` and the plots. Keep a second copy on Google Drive.
4. **GitHub:** push the notebook, `spoof_stream.py`, `results.json` and the plots. Do **not** push `.npy` files, they are too big. You can push `spoof_head.pt` (1 MB).

### 5. What goes into your report from Phase 1
- Dataset table: clips per split and the bonafide/spoof counts
- Architecture: the learned layer-weight head, with the parameter count
- EER, AUC, FAR and FRR on dev and eval, plus the ROC plot
- In-the-Wild result and a one-paragraph explanation of the gap
- The learned layer weights (`softmax(head.w)`), which show which wav2vec2 layers matter most. This makes a good viva point.

### Phase 1 done checklist
- [ ] Both Kaggle datasets saved
- [ ] Final eval EER and AUC written down in `results.json`
- [ ] `score_chunk()` works on a clip you recorded yourself
- [ ] Notebook and model copied to laptop and GitHub

## Summary
After Phase 1 you hold three things: the cached feature files (so you never recompute), `spoof_head.pt` (your trained spoof detector), and a documented evaluation (EER and AUC on dev, eval and In-the-Wild) with plots and a reusable `score_chunk()`. These feed directly into Phase 2 (the NLP intent stream) and later into the fusion network.

Here is the plan I'd follow if I were building this alone with about 10 weeks left. It differs from the synopsis in order, not content: **get a working end-to-end demo early, then improve it**, so you always have something to show.

| Phase | Weeks | What you build | Done when |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Spoof stream** | 1-2 | wav2vec2 features, custom head, EER on ASVspoof and In-the-Wild | `spoof_head.pt` and `score_chunk()` work |
| **2. Walking skeleton** | 3 | FastAPI + WebSocket, mic capture in 2-3 s chunks, React gauge showing the **real spoof score** | You speak into the mic and the gauge moves |
| **3. Intent NLP stream** | 3-5 | LLM-augmented Hindi/Hinglish dataset, Whisper, MuRIL/HingBERT head | Per-category F1 reported, `score_text()` works |
| **4. Voiceprint stream** | 5 | ECAPA-TDNN enrolment and cosine matching | Speaker EER measured, `score_voice()` works |
| **5. Temporal fusion network** | 6-7 | Simulated sequence data, GRU/attention fusion model, static-MLP baseline | Ablation table shows fusion vs each stream vs MLP |
| **6. GenAI agents** | 7-8 | Challenge-response agent and evidence explainer (prompted LLM) | Both produce usable output from the evidence trail |
| **7. Integration + privacy** | 8 | All three streams plus fusion plus agents in one live pipeline; chunk-discard logic; latency tuning | A full live call runs end to end |
| **8. Evaluation** | 9 | Language/accent split, latency, adversarial test, small user rating (5-10 people) | All tables and plots for the report exist |
| **9. Demo, report, viva** | 10 | Demo rehearsal, report, viva prep | You can run the demo twice in a row without failure |

## Why this order

1. **Phase 2 right after Phase 1.** It forces you to solve mic capture, chunking and WebSocket problems while there is still time. This is where most projects get stuck.
2. **NLP data starts in Week 3, not Week 4.** Dataset creation is slow, and it is your biggest risk.
3. **Voiceprint is short.** ECAPA-TDNN is a ready checkpoint, so it takes a few days.
4. **Fusion comes after the three streams exist.** Its training data is built from your streams' outputs.

## Three honest risks

1. **Fusion training data doesn't exist.** No public dataset has call-level sequences of spoof, intent and voiceprint scores. You will simulate them (scam call vs genuine call patterns, with noise) and mix in real sequences from your own test calls. State this clearly in the report. It is acceptable if stated openly.
2. **Hinglish data quality.** Synthetic transcripts can be too clean. Mix in real seed samples and hand-check some.
3. **Live demo fragility.** Speakerphone audio is noisy, and the models trained on clean data may behave differently. Test on real speakerphone recordings from Phase 2 onward, not just at the end.

## What I would cut or shrink
- **Campaign clustering (HDBSCAN):** drop to stretch. Only do it if everything else is finished.
- **User studies:** keep them tiny, 5-10 people with a Likert form.
- **Voiceprint contact enrolment:** support 2-3 enrolled voices, not a full contact system.

## Rules for yourself
- **End of every phase:** commit to GitHub, save outputs as Kaggle datasets, and write the metrics into a running `results.md`. This makes the final report a copy-paste job.
- **End of Week 3 and Week 8:** run the full demo, even if it is ugly.
- **If you fall behind by Week 6,** cut in this order: clustering, user studies, adversarial test.

Want me to start Phase 2 (the walking skeleton) in the same detailed, step-by-step style once you finish Phase 1, or do you want the full Phase 3 data plan first since it needs the earliest start?

## Step 4: Train the classifier head

**Goal:** teach your small network to turn a cached `(13, 768)` feature array into one spoof probability. You use only the **train** features to learn and the **dev** features to decide when to stop. Never touch **eval** here.

### Before you start (check these exist)
- `train_X.npy`, `train_y.npy`, `dev_X.npy`, `dev_y.npy` in `/kaggle/working` (or in your attached features dataset)
- GPU on (training is quick anyway, a few minutes)

### What to do, in order

**1. Load the features**
```python
import numpy as np, torch, torch.nn as nn, json
from torch.utils.data import TensorDataset, DataLoader
from sklearn.metrics import roc_curve, roc_auc_score

device = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
F = "/kaggle/working"          # or "/kaggle/input/rakshavoice-spoof-features"

def make_loader(name, shuffle):
    X = torch.tensor(np.load(f"{F}/{name}_X.npy")).float()
    y = torch.tensor(np.load(f"{F}/{name}_y.npy")).float()
    return DataLoader(TensorDataset(X, y), batch_size=128, shuffle=shuffle), y

tr, ytr = make_loader("train", True)
dv, ydv = make_loader("dev", False)
print(ytr.mean())   # spoof fraction, about 0.9
```

**2. Define the model (your from-scratch head)**
```python
class SpoofHead(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, L=13, D=768):
        super().__init__()
        self.w = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(L))      # learns which wav2vec2 layers matter
        self.net = nn.Sequential(nn.LayerNorm(D), nn.Linear(D, 256),
                                 nn.GELU(), nn.Dropout(0.3), nn.Linear(256, 1))
    def forward(self, x):
        a = torch.softmax(self.w, 0)[None, :, None]
        return self.net((x * a).sum(1)).squeeze(-1)
```

**3. Handle class imbalance.** Train is about 90% spoof, so the model could cheat by always saying "spoof". Weight the rarer class (bonafide) more:
```python
pos_weight = (ytr == 0).sum() / (ytr == 1).sum()      # about 0.11
loss_fn = nn.BCEWithLogitsLoss(pos_weight=pos_weight.to(device))
```
A cleaner alternative is simply to report EER, which is not affected by imbalance, but the weight helps training behave.

**4. EER function and validation helper**
```python
def eer(y, s):
    fpr, tpr, _ = roc_curve(y, s); fnr = 1 - tpr
    i = np.nanargmin(np.abs(fnr - fpr)); return (fpr[i] + fnr[i]) / 2

def evaluate(model, loader):
    model.eval(); s = []
    with torch.no_grad():
        for x, _ in loader:
            s += torch.sigmoid(model(x.to(device))).cpu().tolist()
    return np.array(s)
```

**5. Training loop with checkpointing**
```python
head = SpoofHead().to(device)
opt = torch.optim.Adam(head.parameters(), lr=1e-3, weight_decay=1e-4)
log = {"train_loss": [], "dev_eer": []}
best, patience, bad = 1.0, 5, 0

for ep in range(30):
    head.train(); tot = 0
    for x, y in tr:
        x, y = x.to(device), y.to(device)
        opt.zero_grad()
        loss = loss_fn(head(x), y)
        loss.backward(); opt.step()
        tot += loss.item() * len(x)
    tl = tot / len(ytr)
    e = eer(ydv.numpy(), evaluate(head, dv))
    log["train_loss"].append(tl); log["dev_eer"].append(float(e))
    print(f"epoch {ep:2d}  loss {tl:.4f}  dev EER {e*100:.2f}%")
    if e < best:
        best, bad = e, 0
        torch.save(head.state_dict(), "/kaggle/working/spoof_head.pt")
    else:
        bad += 1
        if bad >= patience: print("early stop"); break
```
**Reading the output:** loss should fall each epoch, and dev EER should fall then flatten. If EER stops improving for 5 epochs, training stops by itself and the best checkpoint stays saved.

**6. Plot the training curve**
```python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
fig, ax = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(9, 3))
ax[0].plot(log["train_loss"]); ax[0].set_title("Train loss")
ax[1].plot([e*100 for e in log["dev_eer"]]); ax[1].set_title("Dev EER (%)")
plt.savefig("/kaggle/working/training_curve.png", dpi=150, bbox_inches="tight")
```

**7. Inspect what the model learned**
```python
head.load_state_dict(torch.load("/kaggle/working/spoof_head.pt"))
lw = torch.softmax(head.w, 0).detach().cpu().numpy()
print(np.round(lw, 3))      # 13 numbers adding to 1: which wav2vec2 layers it relies on
print(sum(p.numel() for p in head.parameters()))   # parameter count, about 0.2 million
```

### What to save from Step 4

| File | Contents | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `spoof_head.pt` | Weights of the best epoch (lowest dev EER) | The trained model, used by everything later |
| `training_curve.png` | Loss and dev EER per epoch | Report figure |
| `train_log.json` | The `log` dict, plus best epoch, best dev EER, learning rate, batch size, dropout, parameter count | Proof of reproducibility and exact settings for the report |
| `layer_weights.npy` | The 13 softmax layer weights | A viva point: which wav2vec2 layers matter for spoofing |

Save the last two with:
```python
json.dump({**log, "best_dev_eer": float(best), "lr": 1e-3, "batch": 128,
           "dropout": 0.3, "params": sum(p.numel() for p in head.parameters())},
          open("/kaggle/working/train_log.json", "w"))
np.save("/kaggle/working/layer_weights.npy", lw)
```

### Common problems

| Symptom | Likely cause and fix |
|---|---|
| Dev EER stuck near 50% | Labels flipped or features not aligned with labels. Check that `X` and `y` were saved from the same protocol order |
| Dev EER is 0% | Data leak, or you loaded the wrong files. Check that dev is not train |
| Loss becomes `nan` | Features contain NaN. Check `np.isnan(X).sum()`, and lower lr to 3e-4 |
| Dev EER rises while loss falls | Overfitting. The checkpointing already protects you. You can also raise dropout to 0.5 |
| `CUDA out of memory` | Lower the batch size to 64 |

Once `spoof_head.pt` and `train_log.json` exist and the dev EER is sensible, Step 4 is done. Step 5 then scores the untouched **eval** set once to get your final reported number.

I'm assuming you've finished **Step 4** (you have `spoof_head.pt` and `train_log.json`), so the next step is **Step 5: evaluate**, followed by **Step 6: the scoring function**. Both go in your second notebook.

## Step 5: Evaluate on the untouched eval set

**Goal:** get the final numbers for your report. You run eval **once**. Never use it to tune anything.

**1. Load the model and eval features**
```python
head = SpoofHead().to(device)
head.load_state_dict(torch.load("/kaggle/working/spoof_head.pt"))
head.eval()

ev, yev = make_loader("eval", False)
yev = yev.numpy()
s_ev = evaluate(head, ev)          # spoof probabilities, one per clip
```

**2. Compute all metrics at the EER point**
```python
from sklearn.metrics import roc_curve, roc_auc_score

def full_metrics(y, s):
    fpr, tpr, thr = roc_curve(y, s)
    fnr = 1 - tpr
    i = np.nanargmin(np.abs(fnr - fpr))
    return {"EER": float((fpr[i] + fnr[i]) / 2),
            "AUC": float(roc_auc_score(y, s)),
            "threshold": float(thr[i]),
            "FAR": float(fpr[i]),    # real speech wrongly flagged as spoof
            "FRR": float(fnr[i])}    # spoof wrongly accepted as real

m_eval = full_metrics(yev, s_ev)
print(m_eval)
```
Note that in this labelling (spoof = 1), FAR here means bonafide flagged as spoof. State your convention in the report.

**3. Plot the ROC curve**
```python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
fpr, tpr, _ = roc_curve(yev, s_ev)
plt.figure(figsize=(4, 4))
plt.plot(fpr, tpr, label=f"AUC={m_eval['AUC']:.3f}")
plt.plot([0, 1], [0, 1], "--", color="gray")
plt.xlabel("False positive rate"); plt.ylabel("True positive rate")
plt.title("Spoof detector ROC (ASVspoof eval)"); plt.legend()
plt.savefig("/kaggle/working/roc_curve.png", dpi=150, bbox_inches="tight")
```

**4. Test generalization on In-the-Wild (no retraining)**
1. Add the In-the-Wild dataset as an input, then run `!find /kaggle/input -maxdepth 3 -name "*.csv"` to find its metadata file.
2. The metadata lists each file and its label (`bona-fide` or `spoof`). Check it with `pd.read_csv(path).head()`.
3. Reuse the `extract` idea. Its audio is not named `.flac`, so write a small variant:
```python
import pandas as pd
meta = pd.read_csv("<path to meta.csv>")
names = meta["file"].tolist()[:3000]
labels = (meta["label"] != "bona-fide").astype(int).values[:3000]
# loop exactly like extract(), loading f"<audio_dir>/{n}" (with its extension)
# save as itw_X.npy and itw_y.npy
```
Take a **random** sample, not just the first rows (`meta.sample(3000, random_state=0)`), so you don't test on only one speaker.
4. Score it with the same head and compute `full_metrics`. Expect a much worse EER, which is the honest result.

**5. Save the results**
```python
m_dev = {"EER": float(best)}                      # from Step 4
json.dump({"dev": m_dev, "eval": m_eval,
           "in_the_wild": m_itw,                  # from step 4 above
           "eval_clips": int(len(yev))},
          open("/kaggle/working/results.json", "w"), indent=2)
```

## Step 6: The scoring function
Define `fe`, `model`, `SR` and `LEN` in this notebook if you haven't (copy them from notebook 1), then write the file that your backend will import:
```python
code = '''
import numpy as np, torch, torch.nn as nn
from pathlib import Path
from transformers import Wav2Vec2Model, Wav2Vec2FeatureExtractor

SR, LEN = 16000, 64000
dev = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
fe = Wav2Vec2FeatureExtractor.from_pretrained("facebook/wav2vec2-base")
w2v = Wav2Vec2Model.from_pretrained("facebook/wav2vec2-base").to(dev).eval()

class SpoofHead(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, L=13, D=768):
        super().__init__()
        self.w = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(L))
        self.net = nn.Sequential(nn.LayerNorm(D), nn.Linear(D,256),
                                 nn.GELU(), nn.Dropout(0.3), nn.Linear(256,1))
    def forward(self, x):
        a = torch.softmax(self.w, 0)[None,:,None]
        return self.net((x*a).sum(1)).squeeze(-1)

head = SpoofHead().to(dev)
head.load_state_dict(torch.load(Path(__file__).parent / "models" / "spoof_head.pt",
                                map_location=dev))
head.eval()

def score_chunk(wav):
    wav = np.asarray(wav, dtype=np.float32)
    if len(wav) < LEN: wav = np.pad(wav, (0, LEN - len(wav)))
    x = fe([wav[:LEN]], sampling_rate=SR, return_tensors="pt").input_values.to(dev)
    with torch.no_grad():
        hs = w2v(x, output_hidden_states=True).hidden_states
        f = torch.stack([h.mean(1) for h in hs], 1).float()
        return torch.sigmoid(head(f)).item()
'''
open("/kaggle/working/spoof_stream.py", "w").write(code)
```

**Test it** on one real and one fake clip:
```python
import librosa
real, _ = librosa.load("<a bonafide .flac>", sr=16000)
fake, _ = librosa.load("<a spoof .flac>", sr=16000)
exec(code)
print(score_chunk(real), score_chunk(fake))   # fake should be higher
```
Later, record a clip of your own voice on your phone and score it too.

## The two Kaggle datasets

These are your cloud backup and your way of moving files between notebooks. Kaggle only keeps `/kaggle/working` files until the session ends, so a dataset is the permanent copy.

### Dataset 1: `rakshavoice-spoof-features`

| | |
|---|---|
| **What it is** | The cached wav2vec2 features from Step 3, plus labels |
| **Why it exists** | Extraction is the slowest part. With this dataset you never run it again |
| **Who uses it** | Any notebook that retrains or re-tunes the spoof head |
| **Size** | Roughly 1-2 GB in total, so keep it on Kaggle only |

**Contents:**
```
rakshavoice-spoof-features/
├── train_X.npy    (N, 13, 768)  float16
├── train_y.npy
├── dev_X.npy
├── dev_y.npy
├── eval_X.npy     (20,000 clips)
├── eval_y.npy
├── itw_X.npy      (3,000 In-the-Wild clips)
└── itw_y.npy
```
Delete `test500_*` before you create it.

**How to create it:**
1. Open notebook 1 (features) and click **Save Version → Save & Run All (Commit)**. Wait for it to finish.
2. Go to the version's **Output** tab and click **New Dataset**.
3. Name it `rakshavoice-spoof-features`.
4. Set visibility to **Private**.
5. Click **Create**.

If In-the-Wild features were made in notebook 2, create the dataset from there, or add `itw_X.npy` and `itw_y.npy` later using **New Version** (see below).

### Dataset 2: `rakshavoice-spoof-model`

| | |
|---|---|
| **What it is** | Your trained model and every result file |
| **Why it exists** | Later notebooks (fusion, evaluation) need the model without retraining |
| **Who uses it** | Phases 5, 7 and 8 on Kaggle, plus anything that needs `score_chunk()` |
| **Size** | A few MB |

**Contents:**
```
rakshavoice-spoof-model/
├── models/
│   ├── spoof_head.pt
│   └── layer_weights.npy
├── results/
│   ├── results.json
│   ├── train_log.json
│   ├── training_curve.png
│   └── roc_curve.png
└── spoof_stream.py
```
This mirrors your laptop's `audio/` folder, so paths work the same way in both places.

**How to create it:**
1. In notebook 2, arrange the files into those folders inside `/kaggle/working`. For example:
```python
!mkdir -p /kaggle/working/models /kaggle/working/results
!mv /kaggle/working/spoof_head.pt /kaggle/working/layer_weights.npy /kaggle/working/models/
!mv /kaggle/working/results.json /kaggle/working/train_log.json /kaggle/working/*.png /kaggle/working/results/
```
2. **Save Version → Save & Run All**, then **Output → New Dataset**.
3. Name it `rakshavoice-spoof-model`, set it **Private**, and click **Create**.

### How to use them in a later notebook
1. Click **Add Input**.
2. Choose the **Your Datasets** tab and add the one you need.
3. Read from `/kaggle/input/rakshavoice-spoof-features/` or `/kaggle/input/rakshavoice-spoof-model/`.

Input folders are read-only, so write any new outputs to `/kaggle/working`.

### Updating a dataset later
Open the dataset page and click **New Version**, then upload the changed files. For example, if you retrain and get a better `spoof_head.pt`, add it as a new version. Kaggle keeps the old versions, so you can go back to them.

### Habits that save you trouble
- Keep both **Private**. They contain your project work.
- Don't put raw call audio or enrolled voice recordings in any dataset. Your privacy design says audio is never stored.
- Later phases get the same treatment: a `rakshavoice-nlp-features`/`-model` pair for Phase 3, and so on.

## Summary
The **features** dataset is the big, rarely changed input that saves you extraction time. The **model** dataset is the small, important output that later phases build on. Create both from your notebook outputs, keep them private, and attach them to future notebooks instead of copying files around.

## Next: Phase 2, the walking skeleton

**Goal:** a live pipe where you speak into the mic, the backend scores 4 s windows with your real `score_chunk()`, and a React gauge moves. This phase is plumbing, not accuracy.

**Where:** on your **laptop**, not Kaggle. Kaggle has no microphone, and your final demo runs locally anyway.

**Honest warning first:** your earlier out-of-domain test gave AUC 0.515 with scores near 1.0. Live mic audio is also out of domain, so the gauge may sit high for everyone, including you. That is expected and **not a bug in Phase 2**. Fixing it (noise/speakerphone augmentation, threshold calibration) comes in Phases 7-8.

### Step 1: Install and check
From `rakshavoice/` with your venv active:
```bash
pip install fastapi "uvicorn[standard]" websockets numpy soundfile
```
Node.js (LTS) must be installed for the frontend. Check with `node -v`.

### Step 2: Chunker (`backend/chunker.py`)
Keeps the last 4 s in memory only and releases a window every 2 s. Nothing is written to disk.
```python
import numpy as np

SR = 16000
WIN = 4 * SR      # model input length
HOP = 2 * SR      # score every 2 s of new audio

class RingBuffer:
    def __init__(self):
        self.buf = np.zeros(0, dtype=np.float32)
        self.new = 0

    def push(self, chunk):
        self.buf = np.concatenate([self.buf, chunk])[-WIN:]
        self.new += len(chunk)
        if len(self.buf) >= WIN and self.new >= HOP:
            self.new = 0
            return self.buf.copy()
        return None

    def clear(self):
        self.buf = np.zeros(0, dtype=np.float32)
        self.new = 0
```
Create an empty `backend/__init__.py` too.

### Step 3: Server (`backend/main.py`)
```python
import asyncio, time
import numpy as np
from fastapi import FastAPI, WebSocket, WebSocketDisconnect
from fastapi.middleware.cors import CORSMiddleware
from audio.spoof_stream import score_chunk
from backend.chunker import RingBuffer

app = FastAPI()
app.add_middleware(CORSMiddleware, allow_origins=["*"],
                   allow_methods=["*"], allow_headers=["*"])

@app.get("/health")
def health():
    return {"ok": True}

@app.websocket("/ws")
async def ws_endpoint(ws: WebSocket):
    await ws.accept()
    buf = RingBuffer()
    loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
    try:
        while True:
            data = await ws.receive_bytes()
            window = buf.push(np.frombuffer(data, dtype=np.float32))
            if window is not None:
                t0 = time.time()
                score = await loop.run_in_executor(None, score_chunk, window)
                await ws.send_json({
                    "stream": "spoof",
                    "score": round(float(score), 4),
                    "latency_ms": int((time.time() - t0) * 1000),
                    "t": time.time(),
                })
                del window            # audio is dropped right after scoring
    except WebSocketDisconnect:
        pass
    finally:
        buf.clear()
```
Run it from the project root:
```bash
uvicorn backend.main:app --port 8000
```
The first start is slow because wav2vec2 loads. Open `http://localhost:8000/health` and you should see `{"ok": true}`.

### Step 4: Test the server without a mic (`backend/test_ws.py`)
Test the backend alone before touching the browser:
```python
import asyncio, json, numpy as np, soundfile as sf, websockets, sys

async def main(path):
    y, sr = sf.read(path, dtype="float32")
    if y.ndim > 1: y = y.mean(1)
    assert sr == 16000, "use a 16 kHz file"
    async with websockets.connect("ws://localhost:8000/ws") as ws:
        for i in range(0, len(y), 4000):          # 0.25 s pieces
            await ws.send(y[i:i+4000].tobytes())
            try:
                print(json.loads(await asyncio.wait_for(ws.recv(), 0.01)))
            except asyncio.TimeoutError:
                pass
        await asyncio.sleep(3)

asyncio.run(main(sys.argv[1]))
```
Run `python backend/test_ws.py some_16k_clip.wav` (at least 6 s long). You should see score messages roughly every 2 s of audio. Check `latency_ms` too: under about 2000 on CPU is fine for a 2 s hop.

### Step 5: Frontend
```bash
npm create vite@latest frontend -- --template react
cd frontend && npm install
```

**`frontend/public/processor.js`** (captures raw mic samples):
```js
class PCMProcessor extends AudioWorkletProcessor {
  constructor() { super(); this.parts = []; this.n = 0; }
  process(inputs) {
    const ch = inputs[0][0];
    if (ch) {
      this.parts.push(new Float32Array(ch)); this.n += ch.length;
      if (this.n >= 4000) {                       // about 0.25 s at 16 kHz
        const out = new Float32Array(this.n); let o = 0;
        for (const p of this.parts) { out.set(p, o); o += p.length; }
        this.port.postMessage(out.buffer, [out.buffer]);
        this.parts = []; this.n = 0;
      }
    }
    return true;
  }
}
registerProcessor("pcm-processor", PCMProcessor);
```

**`frontend/src/App.jsx`** (replace the contents):
```jsx
import { useRef, useState } from "react";

export default function App() {
  const [running, setRunning] = useState(false);
  const [score, setScore] = useState(null);
  const [latency, setLatency] = useState(null);
  const ref = useRef({});

  async function start() {
    const stream = await navigator.mediaDevices.getUserMedia({
      audio: { echoCancellation: false, noiseSuppression: false, autoGainControl: false },
    });
    const ctx = new AudioContext({ sampleRate: 16000 });
    await ctx.audioWorklet.addModule("/processor.js");
    const src = ctx.createMediaStreamSource(stream);
    const node = new AudioWorkletNode(ctx, "pcm-processor");
    const ws = new WebSocket("ws://localhost:8000/ws");
    ws.binaryType = "arraybuffer";
    ws.onmessage = (e) => {
      const m = JSON.parse(e.data);
      setScore(m.score); setLatency(m.latency_ms);
    };
    node.port.onmessage = (e) => {
      if (ws.readyState === 1) ws.send(e.data);
    };
    src.connect(node);
    ref.current = { stream, ctx, ws };
    setRunning(true);
  }

  function stop() {
    const { stream, ctx, ws } = ref.current;
    stream?.getTracks().forEach((t) => t.stop());
    ws?.close(); ctx?.close();
    setRunning(false);
  }

  const pct = score === null ? 0 : Math.round(score * 100);
  const color = pct < 40 ? "#2e9e5b" : pct < 70 ? "#e0a100" : "#d33";

  return (
    <div style={{ maxWidth: 420, margin: "60px auto", fontFamily: "sans-serif" }}>
      <h2>RakshaVoice</h2>
      <button onClick={running ? stop : start}>
        {running ? "Stop" : "Start listening"}
      </button>
      <div style={{ marginTop: 24, background: "#eee", height: 28, borderRadius: 14 }}>
        <div style={{ width: `${pct}%`, height: "100%", background: color,
                      borderRadius: 14, transition: "width .4s" }} />
      </div>
      <p>Synthetic-voice score: <b>{score === null ? "waiting…" : pct + "%"}</b></p>
      <p style={{ color: "#777" }}>Latency: {latency ?? "-"} ms</p>
    </div>
  );
}
```
Run it with `npm run dev`, then open the printed `localhost` address in **Chrome** (it resamples the mic to 16 kHz properly; Firefox often errors). Allow the microphone.

Phase 2 builds the live pipeline: browser mic → 16 kHz PCM chunks → WebSocket → an in-memory 4 s ring buffer → `score_chunk()` → JSON score → React gauge, with no audio ever written to disk. Get it working with a test file first, then the mic, then speakerphone, and log the latency. Once it runs, the next step is Phase 3 (intent NLP stream), and the dataset work for it should begin in parallel now because it is your slowest task.

# Phase 2 in detail: the walking skeleton

I've kept the same six steps in the same order. Each one now has the reason, the exact actions, what you should see, and what can go wrong.

**What you're building**
```
Browser mic ─► 16 kHz PCM chunks ─► WebSocket ─► ring buffer (4 s, RAM only)
                                                      │ every 2 s
                                                      ▼
React gauge ◄── JSON score ◄── WebSocket ◄── score_chunk() (your Phase 1 model)
```

**Where:** your laptop, in a terminal opened in `rakshavoice/`. Use **Chrome**.

**Why:** the server needs FastAPI (web framework), uvicorn (runs it) and websockets (live connection). The frontend needs Node.js to build the React app.

**Actions**

   ```bash

   ```

   ```bash
   node -v
   npm -v
   ```
   Both should print version numbers.
4. Confirm Phase 1 still works before building on it:
   ```bash
   python -c "from audio.spoof_stream import score_chunk; import numpy as np; print(score_chunk(np.zeros(64000)))"
   ```
   It prints a number between 0 and 1. The first run takes a while because wav2vec2 downloads.

**Expected result:** all installs succeed, `node -v` works, and the Phase 1 test prints a number.

**If it fails**

| Problem | Fix |
|---|---|
| `node` not recognized | Close and reopen the terminal after installing |
| `ModuleNotFoundError: audio` | You're not in `rakshavoice/`, or `audio/__init__.py` is missing |
| `FileNotFoundError: spoof_head.pt` | The file must be at `audio/models/spoof_head.pt` |

---

## Step 2: Chunker (`backend/chunker.py`)

**Why:** the browser sends tiny pieces (0.25 s each), but your model needs 4 s windows. The chunker collects pieces in RAM, keeps only the last 4 s, and releases a window every 2 s. This is also your privacy design: nothing touches the disk.

**Actions**
1. Create `backend/__init__.py` as an empty file.
2. Create `backend/chunker.py`:
```python
import numpy as np

SR = 16000
WIN = 4 * SR      # 64000 samples, the model's input length
HOP = 2 * SR      # score after every 32000 new samples

class RingBuffer:
    def __init__(self):
        self.buf = np.zeros(0, dtype=np.float32)
        self.new = 0

    def push(self, chunk):
        self.buf = np.concatenate([self.buf, chunk])[-WIN:]
        self.new += len(chunk)
        if len(self.buf) >= WIN and self.new >= HOP:
            self.new = 0
            return self.buf.copy()
        return None

    def clear(self):
        self.buf = np.zeros(0, dtype=np.float32)
        self.new = 0
```

**How it works**
- `push` appends new audio and trims to the last `WIN` samples, so old audio falls off the front automatically.
- It returns a window only when the buffer is full **and** at least 2 s of new audio has arrived, so windows overlap by 2 s. That overlap makes the gauge update smoothly.
- `clear()` wipes the buffer when the call ends.

**Quick test (no server needed)**
```python
from backend.chunker import RingBuffer
import numpy as np
b = RingBuffer()
for i in range(40):                      # 40 pieces of 0.25 s = 10 s
    w = b.push(np.zeros(4000, dtype=np.float32))
    if w is not None: print("window at piece", i, len(w))
```
Expected: no output for the first 15 pieces (4 s), then a window of length 64000 at piece 15, then every 8 pieces (2 s): pieces 15, 23, 31, 39.

---

## Step 3: Server (`backend/main.py`)

**Why:** this is the bridge between the browser and your model. A **WebSocket** is a connection that stays open, so the browser can stream audio in and the server can push scores out without repeated requests.

**Action:** create `backend/main.py`:
```python
import asyncio, time
import numpy as np
from fastapi import FastAPI, WebSocket, WebSocketDisconnect
from fastapi.middleware.cors import CORSMiddleware
from audio.spoof_stream import score_chunk
from backend.chunker import RingBuffer

app = FastAPI()
app.add_middleware(CORSMiddleware, allow_origins=["*"],
                   allow_methods=["*"], allow_headers=["*"])

@app.get("/health")
def health():
    return {"ok": True}

@app.websocket("/ws")
async def ws_endpoint(ws: WebSocket):
    await ws.accept()
    buf = RingBuffer()
    loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
    try:
        while True:
            data = await ws.receive_bytes()
            window = buf.push(np.frombuffer(data, dtype=np.float32))
            if window is not None:
                t0 = time.time()
                score = await loop.run_in_executor(None, score_chunk, window)
                await ws.send_json({
                    "stream": "spoof",
                    "score": round(float(score), 4),
                    "latency_ms": int((time.time() - t0) * 1000),
                    "t": time.time(),
                })
                del window
    except WebSocketDisconnect:
        pass
    finally:
        buf.clear()
```

**Line-by-line idea**
- `/health` is a simple check that the server is up.
- Each browser connection gets its **own** `RingBuffer`, so sessions don't mix.
- `np.frombuffer(data, dtype=np.float32)` turns the raw bytes from the browser back into numbers. The browser sends 32-bit floats, so the dtype must match.
- `run_in_executor` runs the heavy model in a separate thread, so the server can keep receiving audio while scoring.
- The `finally` block clears the buffer on disconnect. The `{stream, score, ...}` JSON uses the same format you defined for all streams, so Phase 3 and 4 can reuse it.

**Run it** (from `rakshavoice/`, venv active):
```bash
uvicorn backend.main:app --port 8000
```
Wait for `Application startup complete`. The first start is slow because wav2vec2 loads.

**Check:** open `http://localhost:8000/health` in the browser. You should see `{"ok":true}`. Also open `http://localhost:8000/docs` for the auto-generated API page.

Leave this terminal running and open a second terminal for the next steps.

---

## Step 4: Test the server without a mic (`backend/test_ws.py`)

**Why:** debug the backend alone first. If the browser later fails, you'll know the problem is in the frontend.

**Actions**
1. Get a **16 kHz mono WAV** of at least 8 s. Two easy ways:
   - Record your voice on your phone, put the file in `rakshavoice/`, then convert it:
     ```python
     import librosa, soundfile as sf
     y, _ = librosa.load("my_voice.m4a", sr=16000, mono=True)
     sf.write("test16k.wav", y, 16000)
     ```
   - Or download a couple of ASVspoof `.flac` files from Kaggle (one bonafide, one spoof) and convert them the same way. Short ASVspoof clips can be under 8 s, so loop them: `y = np.tile(y, 3)`.
2. Create `backend/test_ws.py`:
```python

    y, sr = sf.read(path, dtype="float32")
    if y.ndim > 1: y = y.mean(1)

    async with websockets.connect("ws://localhost:8000/ws") as ws:

            await ws.send(y[i:i+4000].tobytes())

                print(json.loads(await asyncio.wait_for(ws.recv(), 0.01)))

                pass

```
3. With the server still running, run in the second terminal:
```bash

```

**Expected result:** lines like
```
{'stream': 'spoof', 'score': 0.93, 'latency_ms': 850, 't': 1760000000.1}
```
about every 2 s of audio. Check these:
- The first score only appears after about 4 s of audio.
- `latency_ms` stays under about 2000. If it's higher, scores will lag behind the audio.
- Test your bonafide clip and a spoof clip and note both scores in `results.md`.

**If it fails**

| Problem | Fix |
|---|---|
| `Connect call failed` | The server isn't running or is on another port |
| Assertion error | The file isn't 16 kHz. Reconvert it |
| No output at all | The file is shorter than 4 s |

## Step 5: Frontend

**Why:** the browser must capture the microphone, convert it to the exact format the server expects, and draw the gauge.

### 5a. Create the React app
From `rakshavoice/`:
```bash

```
If it asks about frameworks or variants, pick **React** and **JavaScript**. If `frontend/` already exists with files, choose to overwrite or use an empty folder.

### 5b. The audio processor (`frontend/public/processor.js`)
**Why a separate file:** browsers capture audio in an **AudioWorklet**, a small script that runs on a dedicated audio thread and gets raw samples. It must be a standalone file in `public/`.
```js
class PCMProcessor extends AudioWorkletProcessor {
  constructor() { super(); this.parts = []; this.n = 0; }
  process(inputs) {
    const ch = inputs[0][0];
    if (ch) {
      this.parts.push(new Float32Array(ch)); this.n += ch.length;
      if (this.n >= 4000) {                       // about 0.25 s at 16 kHz
        const out = new Float32Array(this.n); let o = 0;
        for (const p of this.parts) { out.set(p, o); o += p.length; }
        this.port.postMessage(out.buffer, [out.buffer]);
        this.parts = []; this.n = 0;
      }
    }
    return true;
  }
}
registerProcessor("pcm-processor", PCMProcessor);
```
The browser delivers audio in very small blocks (128 samples). This script gathers them into about 4000-sample pieces and sends them to the page.

### 5c. The page (`frontend/src/App.jsx`)
Replace the file's contents:
```jsx
import { useRef, useState } from "react";

export default function App() {

  const [score, setScore] = useState(null);
  const [latency, setLatency] = useState(null);
  const ref = useRef({});

  async function start() {
    const stream = await navigator.mediaDevices.getUserMedia({
      audio: { echoCancellation: false, noiseSuppression: false, autoGainControl: false },
    });
    const ctx = new AudioContext({ sampleRate: 16000 });
    await ctx.audioWorklet.addModule("/processor.js");
    const src = ctx.createMediaStreamSource(stream);
    const node = new AudioWorkletNode(ctx, "pcm-processor");
    const ws = new WebSocket("ws://localhost:8000/ws");
    ws.binaryType = "arraybuffer";
    ws.onmessage = (e) => {
      const m = JSON.parse(e.data);
      setScore(m.score); setLatency(m.latency_ms);
    };
    node.port.onmessage = (e) => {
      if (ws.readyState === 1) ws.send(e.data);
    };
    src.connect(node);
    ref.current = { stream, ctx, ws };
    setRunning(true);
  }

  function stop() {
    const { stream, ctx, ws } = ref.current;
    stream?.getTracks().forEach((t) => t.stop());
    ws?.close(); ctx?.close();
    setRunning(false);
  }

  const pct = score === null ? 0 : Math.round(score * 100);
  const color = pct < 40 ? "#2e9e5b" : pct < 70 ? "#e0a100" : "#d33";

  return (

        {running ? "Stop" : "Start listening"}
      </button>
      <div style={{ marginTop: 24, background: "#eee", height: 28, borderRadius: 14 }}>
        <div style={{ width: `${pct}%`, height: "100%", background: color,
                      borderRadius: 14, transition: "width .4s" }} />
      </div>
      <p>Synthetic-voice score: <b>{score === null ? "waiting…" : pct + "%"}</b></p>
      <p style={{ color: "#777" }}>Latency: {latency ?? "-"} ms</p>
    </div>
  );
}
```

**What matters in this code**
- **Echo cancellation, noise suppression and auto-gain are turned off.** Those filters alter the voice and can hide the artifacts the spoof detector looks for.
- `sampleRate: 16000` makes Chrome resample the mic to the rate your model expects.
- Each piece of audio is sent as raw bytes. Each reply from the server updates the gauge.
- Green below 40%, amber 40-70%, red above 70%. These thresholds are placeholders until you calibrate them in a later phase.
- `stop()` releases the mic and closes the connection.

## Summary
Phase 2 gives you a working end-to-end pipe. The browser captures the microphone as 16 kHz float samples, and an AudioWorklet batches them into 0.25 s pieces. These stream over a WebSocket into an in-memory 4 s ring buffer, and every 2 s your Phase 1 `score_chunk()` scores the window. The score returns as JSON and moves the React gauge, and the audio is discarded immediately after scoring. Build it in the order above: chunker, server, test with a file, then the browser, then record the results. Once this works, Phase 3 (the intent NLP stream) can plug its own score into the same WebSocket message format.

## Phase 2 is done locally, on your laptop

**Why it can't be on Kaggle**

| Needed for Phase 2 | Kaggle |
|---|---|
| Microphone access in the browser | No mic, and notebooks run on a remote server |
| `localhost` WebSocket between browser and server | The server would be in Kaggle's cloud, not reachable from your browser's `localhost` |
| React frontend (`npm run dev`) | Kaggle notebooks are for Python, not running web apps |
| Speakerphone test | Needs your phone and your laptop in the same room |

Phase 1 was training and feature extraction, which need a GPU, so Kaggle suited it. Phase 2 is live plumbing, and your final demo also runs on a laptop, so build it where it will be shown.

## What you need on the laptop
- The `rakshavoice/` folder with `audio/spoof_stream.py` and `audio/models/spoof_head.pt` (downloaded from Kaggle)
- Python venv with the libraries from Step 1
- Node.js
- Chrome
- Internet on the first run only, so wav2vec2 downloads once and is then cached

## If your laptop has no GPU
That's fine. The model runs on CPU, just slower. Expect roughly 1-3 seconds per 4 s window, which still works with the 2 s hop. If latency goes above about 3 s, raise `HOP` to 3 seconds in `chunker.py`. Record the latency you measure in `results.md`, since it is a real result for the report.

## Where Kaggle is still used
Later phases that need training (Phase 3 NLP head, Phase 5 fusion network) go back to Kaggle. You then download the trained model files into your laptop folder, the same way you did for Phase 1. The pattern for the rest of the project is: **train on Kaggle, run live on the laptop.**

## Phase 2 is complete when all six checks below pass

Run them in this order. Each one has a concrete test, so you aren't guessing.

| # | Check | How to test | Pass condition |
|---|---|---|---|

| 2 | **Chunker works** | Run the 40-piece test from Step 2 | Windows of length 64000 appear at pieces 15, 23, 31, 39 |
| 3 | **Backend works without a browser** | `python backend/test_ws.py test16k.wav` | Score messages arrive about every 2 s, first one after about 4 s |
| 4 | **Mic to gauge works** | Open `localhost:5173` in Chrome, click Start, speak for 10 s | Gauge updates about every 2 s, latency is displayed |

| 6 | **Speakerphone test done** | Place a phone call on speaker next to the laptop and let the other person talk for 10 s | Gauge responds, and you have written the value down |

For check 5, from the project root:
```bash
# Mac/Linux
find . -name "*.wav" -newer backend/main.py -not -path "./venv/*" -not -path "./frontend/node_modules/*"
# Windows PowerShell
Get-ChildItem -Recurse -Include *.wav,*.flac | Where-Object { $_.LastWriteTime -gt (Get-Date).AddHours(-1) }
```
It should list nothing, other than your own test clip `test16k.wav` that you made on purpose.

## What does *not* count as a failure
- **High scores for your own real voice.** Live mic audio is out of domain for a model trained on ASVspoof, so the gauge sitting high is expected. Phase 2 tests the pipe, not the accuracy.
- **Slow first score.** The first window needs 4 s of audio plus model loading.

## What *does* count as not done
- Latency regularly above 3 s with no fix (raise `HOP` to 3 s, or note it and move on, but you must have measured it)
- The gauge freezing after a minute or two
- The server crashing when you click Stop or close the tab
- Audio files being created anywhere

- [ ] `results.md` has a Phase 2 section with five numbers: latency, live voice score, bonafide clip score, spoof clip score, speakerphone score
- [ ] `node_modules/` added to `.gitignore`

Phase 2 is done, so the project now has a live pipe from mic to gauge. Next is **Phase 3, the intent NLP stream**. This is your biggest risk, because Hindi/Hinglish scam data doesn't exist publicly, so the data work comes first.

**Goal:** a function `score_text(transcript_chunk)` that returns a 0-1 scam score plus a score per category (urgency, authority impersonation, OTP/PII request, payment pressure), trained on your own labelled Hindi/Hinglish data.

**Where:** data creation and training on **Kaggle**. Whisper and the final `score_text()` run on your **laptop**.

## Phase 3 steps (same order we'll follow)

| Step | What | Where |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Define labels and the data format | Laptop |
| 2 | Collect and hand-label the real seed set | Laptop |
| 3 | Generate synthetic Hinglish data with an LLM | Laptop/Chat |
| 4 | Clean, split and save the dataset | Laptop |
| 5 | Test Whisper on Hindi/Hinglish clips | Kaggle |
| 6 | Train the classifier head on frozen MuRIL/HingBERT | Kaggle |
| 7 | Evaluate (per-category F1) and write `intent_stream.py` | Kaggle, then laptop |

I'll detail Steps 1-3 now, since they decide everything else.

## Step 1: Define labels and data format

**Why:** if your labels are vague, the LLM-generated data will be inconsistent and the classifier will learn noise. Fix the rules before collecting anything.

**The four categories** (a transcript chunk can have several at once, so this is **multi-label**):

| Label | Means | Example (Hinglish) |
|---|---|---|
| `urgency` | Time pressure, threats of consequences | "Aapka account aaj hi block ho jayega" |
| `authority` | Claims to be bank, police, CBI, RBI, telecom officer | "Main CBI officer bol raha hoon" |
| `otp_pii` | Asks for OTP, PIN, CVV, Aadhaar, PAN, card number | "Aapke phone par jo OTP aaya hai wo bataiye" |
| `payment` | Asks to transfer money, pay a fine, install a payment/remote app | "Verification ke liye 5000 rupaye transfer karein" |

A chunk with none of these is a **normal** call (all four labels = 0).

**Data format**, one row per chunk, in a CSV:
```
id, text, lang, urgency, authority, otp_pii, payment, source
1, "Sir aapka KYC expire ho gaya hai, aaj hi update karein", hinglish, 1, 0, 0, 0, real
```
- `lang`: `hindi`, `english` or `hinglish`
- `source`: `real` or `synthetic` (your synopsis requires this flag, and you'll report results separately for the two)
- Write Hindi in **Devanagari** or **romanized**? Pick one rule per file. Whisper outputs Devanagari for Hindi, so include both styles in your data. Mix about 50% each.

Write these rules into a short `nlp/LABELLING_GUIDE.md` with 2 examples per label. You'll use it for your own consistency and for the LLM prompts.

## Step 2: Collect and hand-label the real seed set

**Why:** the synthetic data is only as good as the real examples it copies. Your synopsis names Kaggle Fraud-Call-India as the seed.

**Actions**
1. On Kaggle, search **"Fraud Call India"** and open the dataset. Look at its columns. It usually has call transcripts and a fraud/normal label.
2. Download it, or add it to a Kaggle notebook and load it with pandas.
3. Take a **random sample of about 150-200 rows**, with both fraud and normal examples.
4. Add your four labels by hand in a spreadsheet (Excel or Google Sheets). It takes about an hour, and it is worth it. These are your **gold** examples.
5. Add 30-50 more real-style normal calls yourself (a delivery call, a friend, a bank branch confirming a cheque, a school calling a parent). Normal-call variety matters a lot, or the model will flag everything.
6. Save as `nlp/data/seed_real.csv` with `source = real`.

**Check:** about 40% of the seed should be normal calls, and each category needs at least about 20 positive examples. If a category has fewer, look for more examples or write some yourself.

## Step 3: Generate synthetic Hinglish data with an LLM

**Why:** 200 rows are too few to train on. The LLM expands them into thousands of varied examples.

**How to generate:** use me, or any LLM, in **batches of 20-30 rows per request**, never thousands at once (quality drops). For each batch, paste 6-8 seed examples (a mix of fraud and normal) and use a prompt like:

```
You are generating training data for a scam-call detector for India.
Write 25 NEW short call snippets (1-3 sentences each, as a phone call would sound).

Language mix: 10 Hinglish (romanized), 8 Hindi (Devanagari), 7 English.
Target labels for this batch: {authority + urgency}   ← change per batch
Rules:
- Vary scam types: digital arrest, KYC expiry, electricity bill, courier/customs
  parcel, lottery, SIM block, bank officer, family-member-in-trouble.
- Vary speaker style: polite, aggressive, scripted, hesitant.
- Include Indian names, banks (SBI, HDFC, PNB), and realistic numbers.
- Do NOT repeat sentence structures between rows.
Output as CSV: text,lang,urgency,authority,otp_pii,payment
Here are examples of the style: {paste seed rows}
```

**Plan the batches** so the dataset is balanced:

| Batch type | Rows | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Single-label scams (one per category) | about 4 x 150 | Clean signal |
| Multi-label scams (2-3 labels) | about 500 | Realistic mixes |
| **Normal calls** | about 700-900 | **Include hard negatives**: a real bank branch call, a genuine OTP-delivery reminder, a courier confirming delivery |
| Total target | about 2,000-2,500 | |

**Hard negatives matter most.** If the model only sees obvious normal chit-chat, it will flag any call that mentions "bank" or "OTP". Include genuine calls that mention those words.

**Quality control (required by your synopsis):**
1. Spot-check **10% of every batch** by reading it. Look for rows that are too clean, repeated phrasing, wrong labels, or unnatural Hinglish.
2. Delete duplicates (`df.drop_duplicates("text")`).
3. Set `source = synthetic` on every generated row.
4. Keep a log in `nlp/data/QC_LOG.md`: how many rows you checked, how many were wrong, how many you fixed or removed. This goes into the report.

Save as `nlp/data/synthetic_raw.csv`.

## What you should have after Steps 1-3

```
nlp/
├── LABELLING_GUIDE.md
└── data/
    ├── seed_real.csv        (about 200-250 rows, hand-labelled, source=real)
    ├── synthetic_raw.csv    (about 2,000 rows, source=synthetic)
    └── QC_LOG.md
```

**Time budget:** Step 1 about 1 hour, Step 2 about 2-3 hours, Step 3 about 1-2 days of batching. Start this today, because it's the slowest part of the whole project.

# Phase 3: Intent NLP stream, in detail

**Goal:** a function `score_text(text)` that returns a scam score and a score for each of 4 categories (urgency, authority, otp_pii, payment) on Hindi, English and Hinglish transcript chunks.

**Where each step runs:** data work on laptop, Whisper test and training on Kaggle, the final scoring file on laptop.

```
Phase 3 pipeline

   (Steps 1-4)             (Step 6, cached)                    (Step 6-7)
Whisper (Step 5) turns live speech into the text that score_text() reads
```

## Step 1: Define labels and data format (laptop)

1. Create `nlp/LABELLING_GUIDE.md` containing the 4 definitions, 2 examples per label, and these rules:
   - A row is **multi-label**. A normal call has all four labels at 0.
   - Label what the *caller says*, not what you suspect. "Your account will be blocked today" is `urgency`. Merely mentioning "bank" is not `authority`.
   - A genuine call that asks for no secrets and no payment is normal, even if it mentions OTP or banks.

## Step 4: Clean, split and save (laptop)

**Why the split matters:** if synthetic rows that look alike land in both train and test, your F1 will look great and mean nothing. The real rows are your honest test.

```python
import pandas as pd, re
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import TfidfVectorizer
from sklearn.metrics.pairwise import cosine_similarity

L = ["urgency", "authority", "otp_pii", "payment"]
real = pd.read_csv("nlp/data/seed_real.csv")
syn  = pd.read_csv("nlp/data/synthetic_raw.csv")
df = pd.concat([real, syn], ignore_index=True)

df["key"] = df.text.map(lambda t: re.sub(r"\s+", " ", str(t).lower().strip()))
df = df.drop_duplicates("key").drop(columns="key")
df = df[df[L].isin([0, 1]).all(axis=1)].reset_index(drop=True)
df["any"] = (df[L].sum(axis=1) > 0).astype(int)

def split(d, fr):                       # fr = (train, dev, test) fractions
    tr, rest = train_test_split(d, test_size=1-fr[0], stratify=d["any"], random_state=0)
    dv, te = train_test_split(rest, test_size=fr[2]/(fr[1]+fr[2]), stratify=rest["any"], random_state=0)
    return tr, dv, te

r_tr, r_dv, r_te = split(df[df.source == "real"], (0.4, 0.2, 0.4))
s_tr, s_dv, s_te = split(df[df.source == "synthetic"], (0.8, 0.1, 0.1))
train, dev, test = (pd.concat([r_tr, s_tr]), pd.concat([r_dv, s_dv]), pd.concat([r_te, s_te]))

# remove train rows that are near-copies of test rows
v = TfidfVectorizer(analyzer="char_wb", ngram_range=(3, 5)).fit(df.text)
sim = cosine_similarity(v.transform(train.text), v.transform(test.text)).max(axis=1)
train = train[sim < 0.9]

for n, d in [("train", train), ("dev", dev), ("test", test)]:
    d.drop(columns="any").to_csv(f"nlp/data/{n}.csv", index=False)
    print(n, len(d), d[L].sum().to_dict())
```

**Output:** `train.csv`, `dev.csv`, `test.csv` (about 80/10/10 overall, with the **real** rows making up about 40% of test).

Then on Kaggle create a private dataset **`rakshavoice-nlp-data`** containing these three files.
