Your review also did its job: gold positives moved from `payment` 10 → 4, `authority` 4 → 3, `urgency` 42 → 41, `otp_pii` 30 → 29, which matches the autopay and account-number fixes.

**About "GOLD ERROR RATE 0 of 40":** that only says the AI was right on *easy all-zero rows* that the model also called zero. Zero errors out of 40 still leaves an uncertainty of up to about 9% at 95% confidence, and it says nothing about positives, where you did change labels. In the report, write both: "0 of 40 random negatives changed" and the by-reason table from `merge_gold.py` (how many of the `has_label` and `disagree` rows you changed). Send me that table too, so I can see how reliable the positive labels were.

## The most important thing in your threshold table

Look at `urgency`: at thresholds 0.2, 0.3 and 0.4 the result is P=0.17, R=1.00. That is exactly the share of positives (41 of 249), so the model predicted **everything** as urgent, and at 0.7 it predicted **nothing**. `otp_pii` does the same: everything positive at 0.3, nothing at 0.6. So **all the model's probabilities sit in a narrow band of about 0.4 to 0.65**. The ranking is good (PR-AUC 0.75 and 0.95), but the scores are squashed together, which makes thresholds fragile: a shift of 0.03 flips results. This points to under-training, and your own log agrees: validation mean-AP rose every epoch (0.54 → 0.65 → 0.71 → 0.75) and hadn't flattened when training stopped at epoch 4.

## Fix: three small changes, then retrain (about 30-40 minutes)

In Cell 1, set `EPOCHS = 8`. In Cell 2, change two things:

```python
# 1. cap the positive-class weight lower, so probabilities aren't inflated
pos_weight = torch.tensor(np.clip(neg / np.maximum(pos, 1), 1, 4), dtype=torch.float32).to(dev)

# 2. let the new classification layer learn faster than the pretrained encoder
opt = torch.optim.AdamW([
    {"params": model.enc.parameters(),  "lr": 2e-5},
    {"params": model.head.parameters(), "lr": 1e-3},
], weight_decay=0.01)
```
(Replace the existing `pos_weight = ...` and `opt = ...` lines. Leave the scheduler line as it is.)

I expect, but can't promise, that the scores will spread across 0 to 1 instead of 0.4 to 0.65, and that the thresholds become less fragile. Then rerun the Step D table. If you want to see the narrow band, use this finer sweep instead of the coarse one (valid for both the current model and the retrained one):

```python
for k, l in enumerate(L):
    if yg[:, k].sum() < 5: continue
    print(l)
    for t in np.arange(0.40, 0.72, 0.04):
        pr_ = pg[:, k] >= t; tp = (pr_ & (yg[:, k] == 1)).sum()
        p_, r_ = tp/max(pr_.sum(), 1), tp/max(yg[:, k].sum(), 1)
        print(f"  thr {t:.2f}: P={p_:.2f}  R={r_:.2f}  F1={2*p_*r_/max(p_+r_, 1e-9):.2f}")
```

| `urgency` | P 0.78-0.84, R 0.62-0.69, **F1 about 0.73** at any threshold from 0.40 to 0.56 (PR-AUC 0.85) | Solid. Use **0.50** |
| `otp_pii` | **P = 1.00 at every threshold**, R 0.66 at 0.40, F1 0.79 (PR-AUC 0.96) | Strong precision. Use **0.40**. Recall is the weak spot (cause below) |
| `authority` | P 0.43, R 1.00 (3 positives) | 35 false positives dropped to about 4, but 3 positives prove little |
| Sanity cases | **0.933**, your best yet (v1 was 0.783) | Short-sentence behaviour is solid |

**One caution:** Cell 3 reads `gold_labeled.csv` (the original sheet), so its `authority` and `payment` lines use labels that you have since corrected (the `payment` F1 of 0.57 is based on 4 positives, 3 of which you found were wrong). Ignore those two lines. In Cell 1, change the filename to `gold_verified.csv` so Cell 3 uses the checked labels.

**Counts:** `urgency` 21, `authority` 24, `otp_pii` 3, `payment` 21, 18 all-zero rows. `otp_pii` has only 3 positives, so show it for information only.

**Rules so the result stays honest:**
1. Never train on it. Never tune thresholds on it.
2. Fix the thresholds **before** you look at the results: `[0.50, 0.50, 0.40, 0.50]`.
3. Skim the labels first and tell me if you'd label any row differently. These are my labels, not yours, and the same AI-label caveat applies.

**To run it:** upload `probe_set.csv` to your Kaggle dataset as a new version, refresh the dataset in the notebook, and add this cell after Cell 3 (in the session where `predict` exists):

```python
thr = [0.50, 0.50, 0.40, 0.50]       # fixed before looking at the probe set
probe = pd.read_csv(f"{DATA}/probe_set.csv")
pp, yp = predict(probe), probe[L].values.astype(int)
print("PROBE SET (never used in training or in choosing thresholds)")
for k, l in enumerate(L):
    pr_ = pp[:, k] >= thr[k]
    p, r, f, _ = precision_recall_fscore_support(yp[:, k], pr_, average="binary", zero_division=0)
    print(f"{l:10s} P={p:.2f} R={r:.2f} F1={f:.2f}  PR-AUC={average_precision_score(yp[:, k], pp[:, k]):.2f}  positives={int(yp[:, k].sum())}")
print()
for k in [1, 3]:
    for lang in ["english", "hinglish", "hindi"]:
        m = (probe.lang == lang).values
        _, _, f, _ = precision_recall_fscore_support(yp[m, k], pp[m, k] >= thr[k], average="binary", zero_division=0)
        print(f"{L[k]:10s} {lang:9s} F1={f:.2f}")
print()
for k, l in enumerate(L):
    wrong = (pp[:, k] >= thr[k]) != (yp[:, k] == 1)
    for t, s, y in zip(probe.text[wrong], pp[wrong, k], yp[wrong, k]):
        print(f"{l:10s} {'MISS' if y == 1 else 'FP  '} {s:.2f} | {t[:110]}")
```

It prints per-label results, per-language F1 for `authority` and `payment` (each language has only 7-8 positives, so treat these as rough), and every mistake.

## Step 2: One more retrain, to fix `otp_pii` recall

Your `otp_pii` recall tops out at 0.66 even at the lowest threshold, while precision is 1.00. I checked your training labels: **124 chunks in fraud calls that plainly ask for a password, CVV or PAN number are labelled 0**, because the original regex only caught some phrasings. Examples: "To unlock your account, I just need your username and password", "We really need that CVV to proceed", "I'll need your PAN card number". The model is learning to ignore them.

In `combine_training_data.py`, add this beside the other patterns:
```python
CUE = re.compile(r"\b(?:provide|give|tell|share|confirm|need|require|enter|read out|send|batao|bataiye|batayiye|dena|dijiye|bata dijiye|verify)\b|बताइए|दीजिए|बता दीजिए", re.I)
```
and inside `clean()` replace the `d["otp_pii"] = ...` line with:
```python
req_secret = yes & d.text.str.contains(SECRET) & d.text.str.contains(CUE) & ~d.text.str.contains(WARN)
d["otp_pii"] = (keep_otp | upi_req | pay_secret | req_secret).astype(int)
```
Rerun the script. The `otp_pii` ICFD positives should rise from about 203 to roughly 320. **Read 10 of the newly added rows** before uploading, to confirm they are real requests (some could be false hits like "an OTP was sent, please confirm" — if more than 2 of 10 look wrong, tell me). Then upload the new `train_combined.csv` and `val_clean.csv` as a new dataset version, retrain with the same notebook (8 epochs is fine, the best epoch is saved), and rerun the gold and probe cells.

## Step 3: Freeze Phase 3

After that retrain, stop tuning. Phase 3 is done when you have:
- `urgency` and `otp_pii` numbers on the verified gold set, and `authority` and `payment` on the probe set
- The final thresholds in `config.json`
- `model_fp16.pt`, `config.json` and `results.json` downloaded

Then the next work is wiring the fine-tuned model into `intent_stream.py` (it still loads the old frozen-embedding head) and testing it with Whisper on your RTX 3050. After that comes Phase 4 (voiceprint).

| `authority` | 0.95 | 0.88 | **0.91** | 0.94 | 24 | Strong, and it holds on scenarios it never saw (English 0.93, Hinglish 0.88, Hindi 0.93). This is your real evidence for `authority`, much better than the 3-positive gold |

| `urgency` | 0.52 | 0.81 | **0.63** | 0.84 | 21 | Weak precision on this set |
| `otp_pii` | 1.00 | 1.00 | 1.00 | 1.00 | 3 | Only 3 positives, so not meaningful |

**What the mistakes show (I only saw the first part of the list):**
- **`urgency` false positives:** the model fires on scam-style demands that have no time pressure ("deposit fifty thousand rupees to start trading", "pay a registration fee", "you must come to the station"), at scores of 0.56-0.84. It has learned "scam-sounding demand means urgent". One caveat: "you must come to the station" is borderline, and I labelled it 0 because it has no speed element, so part of this gap is label strictness, not just model error.
- **`payment` false positives:** Hindi `authority` rows (crime branch, Interpol) score 0.55-0.70 for `payment`. My batch-3 training rows often had `authority` and `payment` together, so the model links them.
- **`payment` misses:** the QR-code/UPI-PIN trick (0.17) and "link par jaakar bhar dijiye" / "भर दीजिए" for a challan (0.06). Those are gaps in how payment verbs and scam types are covered in training.

## The retrain results (new model, after the `otp_pii` fix)

| Check | Result | Reading |
|---|---|---|
| Training rows | 6,421 (was 6,313) | About 108 extra rows, matching the roughly 120 I expected, so the `otp_pii` patch applied |
| Training curve | Best validation mean-AP 0.759 at epoch 7 (saved) | Fine |
| `otp_pii` on gold | **P 1.00, R 0.83, F1 0.91, PR-AUC 1.0** (was R 0.66, F1 0.79) | The recall ceiling is lifted, and precision held |
| `urgency` on gold | P 0.82, R 0.66, F1 0.73 | Same as before |
| Sanity cases | **0.925** | Still far above v1 (0.783) |

## Three cautions before you trust those numbers

1. **Cell 3 still reads the old gold file.** Its positive counts (41 / 3 / 29 / 4) are the pre-verification sheet. The verified sheet has `payment` at 1, so ignore the `payment` and `authority` lines in Cell 3. Change the filename in Cell 1 to `gold_verified.csv`.
2. **The `otp_pii` threshold of 0.84 was picked automatically from the weak validation labels.** It is probably higher than it needs to be. PR-AUC of 1.0 on gold means a good threshold exists somewhere lower, and recall could rise further. I want the sweep before choosing.
3. **Your gold is "in-distribution".** It comes from the same ICFD source as your training data, so scores on it are optimistic. The probe set is the harder, independent check, and I'd report both.

## Run this one cell (gets me everything missing)

Run it in the session after retraining. It prints the gold sweeps for the two labels that have enough positives, and the probe on the **new** model with the same fixed thresholds as before, so the before/after comparison is fair:

```python
gold = pd.read_csv(f"{DATA}/gold_verified.csv"); yg = gold[L].values.astype(int); pg = predict(gold)
for k in [0, 2]:
    print(f"\n{L[k]}  gold positives={yg[:, k].sum()}  PR-AUC={average_precision_score(yg[:, k], pg[:, k]):.2f}")
    for t in np.arange(0.20, 0.96, 0.05):
        pr_ = pg[:, k] >= t; tp = (pr_ & (yg[:, k] == 1)).sum()
        p_, r_ = tp / max(pr_.sum(), 1), tp / max(yg[:, k].sum(), 1)
        print(f"  thr {t:.2f}: P={p_:.2f} R={r_:.2f} F1={2*p_*r_/max(p_+r_, 1e-9):.2f}")

thr = [0.50, 0.50, 0.40, 0.50]       # same fixed thresholds as the first probe run
probe = pd.read_csv(f"{DATA}/probe_set.csv"); pp = predict(probe); yp = probe[L].values.astype(int)
print("\nPROBE SET, new model (thresholds fixed in advance:", thr, ")")
for k, l in enumerate(L):
    pr_ = pp[:, k] >= thr[k]
    p, r, f, _ = precision_recall_fscore_support(yp[:, k], pr_, average="binary", zero_division=0)
    print(f"{l:10s} P={p:.2f} R={r:.2f} F1={f:.2f}  PR-AUC={average_precision_score(yp[:, k], pp[:, k]):.2f}  positives={int(yp[:, k].sum())}")
for k in [1, 3]:
    for lang in ["english", "hinglish", "hindi"]:
        m = (probe.lang == lang).values
        _, _, f, _ = precision_recall_fscore_support(yp[m, k], pp[m, k] >= thr[k], average="binary", zero_division=0)
        print(f"{L[k]:10s} {lang:9s} F1={f:.2f}")
for k, l in enumerate(L):
    wrong = (pp[:, k] >= thr[k]) != (yp[:, k] == 1)
    for t, s, y in zip(probe.text[wrong], pp[wrong, k], yp[wrong, k]):
        print(f"{l:10s} {'MISS' if y == 1 else 'FP  '} {s:.2f} | {t[:110]}")
```

I tested the cell logic with fake predictions, so errors would only come from your notebook state (it needs `predict`, `DATA` and the `probe_set.csv` and `gold_verified.csv` files uploaded to your dataset).

| `payment` | F1 0.67 on the probe (21 positives) | **Moderate to weak**. It misses QR-code tricks and some Hindi payment verbs |
| Short-sentence sanity cases | 0.925 accuracy (your original model: 0.783) | **Good** |

**What "very bad" would look like:** F1 below about 0.5 on a decently sized test set, PR-AUC near chance, or sanity accuracy under 0.7. You aren't there on any label. Your PR-AUC values (0.84 to 1.0) mean the model ranks scam chunks above normal ones well. The weakness is mostly in where the line is drawn (thresholds and fuzzy label definitions), not in what the model has learned.

## Why you shouldn't over-trust the good numbers
- **Small test sets.** With 21 to 42 positives, an F1 of 0.73 could easily be 0.60 to 0.85 in reality.
- **The gold sheet came from the same ICFD source as your training data**, so it is the easy test. The probe set is the harder, independent one, and it gave lower numbers for `urgency` and `payment`.
- **The labels were AI-assisted and only partly human-checked**, and the probe labels are mine. Say that plainly in the report.
- I haven't seen the probe results for the new model yet.

## Why it is good enough to move on
1. **The fusion network can use scores, not yes/no flags.** You can feed it the raw probabilities from each stream, so ranking quality (the good PR-AUC) matters more than a hard threshold. A noisy `urgency` score is also less harmful when it is combined with the spoof and voiceprint streams, instead of triggering an alert on its own.
2. **Your project is graded on method and honesty** as much as on the final F1: a documented data pipeline, an explicit weak-label audit, a verified gold set, an independent probe set, per-language results and stated limitations. You already have all of that.
3. **Your timeline.** The other phases (voiceprint, fusion, GenAI agents, integration, evaluation) still need doing, and another 0.05 of F1 here is worth less than finishing them.

## What would make it stronger for the report (cheap, optional)
- A **TF-IDF baseline** scored on the same gold and probe sets, so you can show the fine-tuned model adds value. This takes about 15 minutes.
- A short **limitations paragraph**: `urgency` over-fires on demands without time pressure, `payment` misses some scam types and Hindi verbs, and `authority`/`payment` were evaluated on a small synthetic probe because real-data positives were too few.

## Short answer: freeze Phase 3 now

I'd call these results good enough to freeze, with their limits stated plainly in the report.

## First, a correction on my side
I told you the first probe printout was from the old model. The probe output you just sent is **identical** to it, including the same mistakes, so both runs were on the new model. That means there is no before/after comparison on the probe. It doesn't matter, because the gold sweeps show the improvement clearly:

| `otp_pii` on verified gold, threshold 0.40 | Before the label fix | After |
|---|---|---|
| Precision | 1.00 | 1.00 |
| Recall | 0.66 | **0.97** |

## What the sweeps tell you

| Label | What I see | Threshold I'd use |
|---|---|---|
| `otp_pii` | Flat plateau from 0.30 to 0.50: P = 1.00, R = 0.97, F1 = 0.98. PR-AUC 1.00. The automatic 0.84 was far too high | **0.40** (middle of the plateau) |
| `urgency` | Gold F1 drifts from 0.80 (thr 0.20) to 0.72 (thr 0.50). The probe, however, has false positives at 0.54-0.84 | **0.50**. The gold F1 cost is small, and precision matters more for a user-facing alert |
| `authority` | Probe F1 0.91 at 0.5 | **0.50** |
| `payment` | Probe F1 0.67 at 0.5 | **0.50** |

Thresholds come from the gold set and from decisions made before the probe. I did not tune on the probe.

## The remaining errors fall into three patterns

| Pattern | Evidence | What it means |
|---|---|---|
| **`payment` fires on authority threats** | 9 of the 15 payment false positives are authority rows (GST, PF office, crime branch, Interpol), scoring 0.55-0.90, in all three languages | Training rows where authority and payment appear together taught the association |
| **`urgency` fires on scam-style demands with no time pressure** | 14 false positives such as "deposit fifty thousand rupees to start trading" (0.54-0.84) | The model learned "scam demand = urgent" |
| **Unseen regulator and scam type** | All three SEBI rows are missed (scores 0.00-0.02), and the traffic-challan "pay via link right now" rows are missed (0.06-0.40) | `authority` generalised to UIDAI, GST, immigration, PF, municipal and Interpol, but not to a regulator it never saw |

Two of the `payment` "misses" are also label-debatable: the three QR-code-plus-UPI-PIN rows are clearly an `otp_pii` request, and whether they count as `payment` is arguable. Without them, payment recall would be about 0.83 instead of 0.71.

These can be fixed with one more data batch (authority threats **without** payment, payment demands **without** urgency, more regulators and payment phrasings). I'd do that later if time allows. After using the probe failures to design it, the probe would no longer be a clean test, so I'd keep it as a development set and write a fresh one for any final claim.

## Step 2: Put the model into your project (laptop)

Create `nlp/models/intent_v4/` and put `model_fp16.pt` and `config.json` in it. Make sure `nlp/__init__.py` exists (empty file). Then save these two files. I syntax-checked them, but I couldn't run them with torch here, so expect to fix a small error on first run.

**`nlp/intent_stream.py`**
```python
import json
from pathlib import Path
import torch, torch.nn as nn
from transformers import AutoConfig, AutoModel, AutoTokenizer

ROOT = Path(__file__).parent / "models" / "intent_v4"
cfg = json.load(open(ROOT / "config.json"))
LABELS, THR, MAXLEN = cfg["labels"], cfg["thresholds"], cfg["max_length"]
DEV = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
DTYPE = torch.float16 if DEV == "cuda" else torch.float32


class Net(nn.Module):                       # same architecture as the Kaggle training notebook
    def __init__(self, encoder):
        super().__init__()
        self.enc = encoder
        self.drop = nn.Dropout(0.1)
        self.head = nn.Linear(encoder.config.hidden_size, len(LABELS))

    def forward(self, ids, mask):
        h = self.enc(input_ids=ids, attention_mask=mask).last_hidden_state
        m = mask.unsqueeze(-1).float()
        pooled = (h * m).sum(1) / m.sum(1)                       # mean pooling
        return self.head(self.drop(pooled.to(self.head.weight.dtype)))


_tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(cfg["encoder"])
_model = Net(AutoModel.from_config(AutoConfig.from_pretrained(cfg["encoder"])))   # architecture only
_sd = torch.load(ROOT / "model_fp16.pt", map_location="cpu")
_model.load_state_dict({k: v.float() for k, v in _sd.items()})
_model = _model.to(DEV).to(DTYPE).eval()


@torch.no_grad()
def _probs(texts):
    enc = _tok(texts, padding=True, truncation=True, max_length=MAXLEN, return_tensors="pt").to(DEV)
    return torch.sigmoid(_model(enc["input_ids"], enc["attention_mask"]).float()).cpu().numpy()


def score_text(text):
    """Returns one stream message in the shared format {stream, score, labels, evidence}."""
    p = _probs([text])[0]
    labels = {l: round(float(v), 4) for l, v in zip(LABELS, p)}
    fired = [l for l, v, t in zip(LABELS, p, THR) if v >= t]
    return {"stream": "intent",
            "score": round(float(p.max()), 4),          # simple aggregate; the fusion network will learn its own weighting
            "labels": labels,
            "evidence": fired}


score_text_stream = score_text                           # alias used by the backend
```

**`nlp/asr.py`** (install first with `pip install faster-whisper`)
```python
import numpy as np
from faster_whisper import WhisperModel
try:
    _m = WhisperModel("small", device="cuda", compute_type="int8_float16")
except Exception:
    _m = WhisperModel("small", device="cpu", compute_type="int8")     # fallback if CUDA libraries are missing

def transcribe(wav, language=None):
    """wav: float32 numpy array, 16 kHz mono. language: None (auto), 'hi' or 'en'."""
    wav = np.asarray(wav, dtype=np.float32)
    segs, _ = _m.transcribe(wav, language=language, beam_size=1, vad_filter=True)
    return " ".join(s.text.strip() for s in segs).strip()
```

```
## Phase 3: Intent stream (fine-tuned MuRIL, chunk level)
- Training: ICFD chunks (strict relabel) + 246 synthetic rows (x4), 6,421 rows; 8 epochs, best epoch by val mean-AP
- Verified gold (249 rows; 110 reviewed by hand, 4 changed): otp_pii P 1.00 R 0.97 (thr 0.40), PR-AUC 1.00;

- Limitations: gold labels AI-assisted and partly human-checked; gold from the same source as training (optimistic);
  authority/payment have too few real-data positives; urgency over-fires on scam demands; unseen regulators (SEBI) missed
```

**Step 4 matters most.** Your own `PHASE3_REPORT.md` shows Whisper was tested on only 4 recordings, and one transcribed as "Um". Your classifier is trained on clean text, but the live system will feed it Whisper output from noisy speakerphone audio. That is the real test, and your report needs the latency numbers. Put your clips in `nlp/test_clips/` (the `.gitignore` already keeps `*.wav` out of GitHub) and save this as `nlp/test_end_to_end.py`:

```python
import glob, os, time, librosa
from nlp.asr import transcribe
from nlp.intent_stream import score_text

for f in sorted(glob.glob("nlp/test_clips/*.wav")):
    y, _ = librosa.load(f, sr=16000, mono=True)
    t0 = time.time(); text = transcribe(y); t1 = time.time()
    r = score_text(text); t2 = time.time()
    print(f"{os.path.basename(f):22s} audio {len(y)/16000:4.1f}s | asr {t1-t0:4.1f}s | nlp {1000*(t2-t1):4.0f}ms | {r['evidence']} | {text[:70]}")
```

```python
import subprocess, numpy as np
def used():
    return int(subprocess.check_output(["nvidia-smi", "--query-gpu=memory.used", "--format=csv,noheader,nounits"]).decode().split()[0])
base = used(); print("baseline (desktop apps) MiB:", base)
from audio.spoof_stream import score_chunk;  score_chunk(np.zeros(64000, dtype=np.float32)); a = used(); print("after spoof model (wav2vec2):", a - base, "MiB")
from nlp.intent_stream import score_text;    score_text("hello there");                       b = used(); print("after intent model (MuRIL):  ", b - a, "MiB")
from nlp.asr import transcribe;              transcribe(np.zeros(64000, dtype=np.float32));   c = used(); print("after Whisper small:         ", c - b, "MiB")
print("TOTAL in use now:", c, "MiB of 4096")
```

**3. Replace `nlp/test_end_to_end.py`** with this version. It excludes start-up time, checks each clip against what it should trigger, and prints the labels when something looks wrong:
```python
import glob, os, time, librosa
from nlp.asr import transcribe
from nlp.intent_stream import score_text

EXPECT = {"scam_otp": "otp_pii", "scam_police": "authority", "scam_pay": "payment"}
LANG = None                       # run once with None, once with "hi" and keep the better one
score_text("warm up"); transcribe(librosa.tone(440, sr=16000, duration=2))     # exclude start-up cost from timings

ok = n = 0
for f in sorted(glob.glob("nlp/test_clips/*.wav")):
    name = os.path.basename(f)
    y, _ = librosa.load(f, sr=16000, mono=True)
    t0 = time.time(); text = transcribe(y, language=LANG); t1 = time.time()
    r = score_text(text); t2 = time.time()
    exp = next((v for k, v in EXPECT.items() if k in name), None)       # None = normal call, nothing should fire
    got = r["evidence"]
    good = (exp in got) if exp else (len(got) == 0)
    n += 1; ok += good
    print(f"{'OK   ' if good else 'CHECK'} {name:26s} audio {len(y)/16000:4.1f}s asr {t1-t0:4.2f}s nlp {1000*(t2-t1):3.0f}ms "
          f"fired={got} | {text[:80]}")
    if not good: print("      labels:", r["labels"])
print(f"\n{ok}/{n} clips behaved as expected")
```
Run it twice (`LANG = None`, then `LANG = "hi"`) on the Hinglish and Hindi clips and keep the setting with the better transcripts. Run from `rakshavoice/`.

I compile-checked all three scripts, but couldn't run them with a microphone or GPU here, so tell me about any error.

## What to expect and how to read it
- This tests the **pipeline** (Whisper fidelity, latency, speakerphone noise), not the classifier fairly, because these lines are similar to my synthetic training rows. A clip marked `CHECK` usually means Whisper garbled the words, so read the printed transcript to see which.
- The Devanagari lines are the riskiest: Whisper may transcribe Hindi in Devanagari or romanized form, and the model has seen both, but expect some misses.
- Write down the failure patterns for the report (for example "speakerphone Hindi clips transcribed poorly"), because Whisper's accuracy on Indian speech is part of your language-fairness analysis.

## Verdict: the classifier is fine, and Whisper is the weak link for Hindi and Hinglish

### What your numbers show

| Check | Result | Reading |
|---|---|---|
| **Normal calls** (18 clips: delivery, EMI, "never share your OTP") | **18/18 no alert**, in every language, even with garbled transcripts | No false alarms, including on warnings that mention OTP |
| **Scam calls** (18 clips) | 8/18 detected | English 5/6, Hinglish 2/6, Hindi 1/6 |
| Overall, auto language | 26/36 | |
| Overall, forced `LANG="hi"` | 22/36 | Worse, so don't force Hindi |

All 10 failures trace to the **transcript**, not the model. The key words are mangled before the classifier sees them: "OTP" becomes अटेपी / अदिपी / अड़े पी, "फीस" becomes फीगे, "UPI" becomes यूप्या, "पाँच हज़ार" becomes पाँ च़जा. Two more problems: one far-field Hindi clip came out in **Urdu script**, and one English far-field clip lost most of its words ("So I am calling you justreceived"). The `authority` clips mostly worked because "CBI" is recognised.

A confirmation of the probe finding on real audio: the English police clip also fired `payment` (the authority-to-payment false positive).

### Latency
- **Auto language:** 0.25-0.64 s per 8 s clip, typically 0.3-0.5 s. That is real-time.
- **One outlier:** 4.23 s on a garbled far-field clip. That is likely Whisper re-decoding after a poor first attempt, which `temperature=0.0` below prevents.
- **Forced `hi`:** 3.8-11 s on English clips, because Whisper hallucinated Hindi script for English speech. Another reason not to force it.

### VRAM (measured)
| Step | MiB |
|---|---|
| Desktop apps (baseline) | 961 |
| + wav2vec2 spoof model | 592 |
| + MuRIL intent model | **1,238** |
| + Whisper small | 174 |
| **Total** | **2,965 of 4,096** |

It fits, with about 1.1 GB spare. The MuRIL number is higher than its weights need (about 0.5 GB in fp16). The likely cause is that `intent_stream.py` moves the model to the GPU in full precision and *then* converts it, which leaves the freed memory reserved. Converting on the CPU first should save several hundred MiB. I haven't measured this, so check it.

## What to do: three quick experiments on the same 36 clips (about 30 minutes)

**Experiment 0: memory fix.** In `nlp/intent_stream.py` change the last model line to convert before moving, and clear the cache:
```python
_model = _model.to(DTYPE).to(DEV).eval()      # was: _model.to(DEV).to(DTYPE).eval()
if DEV == "cuda": torch.cuda.empty_cache()
```
Rerun `vram_check.py` and note the new MuRIL number.

**Experiments 1-3: improved ASR.** Replace `nlp/asr.py` with this version. It uses no fallback re-decoding, adds a prompt containing your domain vocabulary (which biases Whisper toward the right words and Latin script), and re-runs with Hindi when Whisper guesses an odd language such as Urdu:

```python
import numpy as np
from faster_whisper import WhisperModel

MODEL = "small"               # experiment 3: "Hub84/faster-whisper-hinglish-prime"
USE_PROMPT = True             # experiment 1 = False, experiment 2 = True
PROMPT = ("Sir, aapka account block ho jayega. Please apna OTP, UPI PIN, CVV ya password bataiye. "
          "Main CBI officer bol raha hoon, arrest warrant jaari hua hai. "
          "Processing fee ke liye paanch hazaar rupaye transfer kijiye. KYC, Aadhaar, PAN, EMI, bank.") if USE_PROMPT else None

try:
    _m = WhisperModel(MODEL, device="cuda", compute_type="int8_float16")
except Exception:
    _m = WhisperModel(MODEL, device="cpu", compute_type="int8")


def _run(wav, language):
    segs, info = _m.transcribe(wav, language=language, beam_size=1, vad_filter=True,
                               temperature=0.0,                    # no slow re-decoding fallbacks
                               condition_on_previous_text=False, initial_prompt=PROMPT)
    return " ".join(s.text.strip() for s in segs).strip(), info.language


def transcribe(wav, language=None):
    """wav: float32 numpy array, 16 kHz mono."""
    wav = np.asarray(wav, dtype=np.float32)
    text, lang = _run(wav, language)
    if language is None and lang not in ("en", "hi"):              # e.g. Urdu-script guesses on far-field Hindi
        text, _ = _run(wav, "hi")
    return text
```
(I syntax-checked it, but it is untested on audio.) In `test_end_to_end.py` keep `LANG = None`, and run the 36 clips three times:

| Run | `MODEL` | `USE_PROMPT` | What it tests |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `"small"` | `False` | Effect of the no-fallback settings alone |
| 2 | `"small"` | `True` | Effect of the domain prompt |
| 3 | `"Hub84/faster-whisper-hinglish-prime"` | try `True` and `False` | A Hinglish-tuned Whisper |

For run 3: I found a CTranslate2 conversion of the Oriserve "Hindi2Hinglish-Prime" model on Hugging Face. Its page says it is quantised to about 1.5 GB for 4 GB GPUs and that it is Apache 2.0 licensed. It is a community conversion, and I haven't tested it on your audio. The name suggests it outputs **romanized Hinglish**, which matches how most of your classifier's training text is written. If it outputs Devanagari or garbage with `language=None`, try `language="en"` in `test_end_to_end.py`, which is what a similar romanized Hinglish model's page uses. It needs the VRAM fix from Experiment 0 first, and you should rerun `vram_check.py` with it loaded. A smaller alternative with the same idea is `abhishekgautamm/whisper-tiny-hinglish-ct2` (tiny and fast, but its own page says expect errors on noisy audio).

**Pick the winner by this rule, decided in advance:** the highest clips-OK count out of 36, provided latency stays under about 1 s per 8 s clip and total VRAM stays under about 3,800 MiB.

## If no ASR option gets Hindi clips working
The fallback is training the classifier on noisy transcripts: take the observed Whisper errors (अटेपी for OTP, फीगे for फीस, यूप्या for UPI) and corrupt the Devanagari training text the same way, so the model learns to recognise the mangled versions. That is a real improvement but another retrain, so only do it if the experiments above fail.

## What this means for your report
This is a valuable honest result: **the intent stream's end-to-end weakness on Hindi and Hinglish comes from ASR, not from the classifier**, and your fusion design softens it because the spoof and voiceprint streams don't depend on transcripts. It supports the synopsis claim about language and accent evaluation. Log it in `results.md`:
- Normal calls 0/18 false alarms, scam detection 5/6 English, 2/6 Hinglish, 1/6 Hindi (before ASR changes)
- Whisper small latency 0.3-0.5 s per 8 s clip; 4.23 s worst case before the no-fallback setting
- VRAM total 2,965 MiB of 4,096

1. The new `vram_check.py` printout after Experiment 0.
2. For each of the three ASR runs, just the last line (`X/36 clips behaved as expected`), plus 3-4 `CHECK` lines from the best run.
3. The ASR latency range for each run (min and max `asr` seconds).

With those I'll tell you which setup to keep, and we can close Phase 3.

## Verdict: Hub84 is clearly the best ASR, and the remaining failures are mostly the classifier's fault

### The four ASR runs, compared

| Run | Setup | Clips OK (of 36) | What happened |
|---|---|---|---|
| Original (from before) | small, default settings | 26 | Garbled Hindi and Hinglish |
| 1 | small + prompt | **30** | English good, Hinglish and Hindi romanized and mostly readable. Two English clips came out as Devanagari and Telugu junk |
| 2 | small, no prompt | **21** | Worst. Repetition loops ("तो तो तो…", "आप आप आप…") on every English scam clip, 1.6-2.1 s each |
| 3 | Hub84 + prompt | **31** | Clean transcripts in every language |
| 4 | Hub84, no prompt | **31** | Identical result to run 3 |

I told you `temperature=0.0` would prevent slow re-decoding. For Whisper small **without** the prompt it made things worse, because it removes the fallback that stops repetition loops. That was a mistake on my part. The prompt rescues it, but Hub84 doesn't need any of it.

**Decision:** in `nlp/asr.py` set `MODEL = "Hub84/faster-whisper-hinglish-prime"` and `USE_PROMPT = False` (same score, and no risk of prompt text leaking into transcripts). It outputs **romanized** text even for Hindi speech ("Aapka khaata band ho jaega. Abhi apna OTP bataie?"), which matches what your classifier was mostly trained on. English clips stay English.

### Latency and VRAM
- **Hub84:** 0.7-1.1 s per 8 s clip, very consistent, so about 0.5 s for a 4 s window. **Intent model:** 5-52 ms (you saw 10-35).
- **My memory fix worked:** MuRIL went from **1,238 MiB to 301 MiB**.
- **Total in use: 2,370 MiB of 4,096**, but I can't tell which Whisper model was loaded for that reading. Hub84 is a bigger model (the page says about 1.5 GB). **Please rerun `vram_check.py` with Hub84 set.** My rule was to stay under about 3,800 MiB. If it's higher, closing Edge, Brave and WhatsApp frees roughly 1 GB (your baseline is about 1 GB), and `compute_type="int8"` is another thing to try.

### What the test now shows (Hub84 runs)

| | Result |
|---|---|
| Normal calls | **18/18 no false alarm** |
| Scam calls detected | **13/18** (was 8/18): English 5/6, Hinglish 4/6, Hindi 4/6 |
| Remaining 5 failures | 1 ASR miss + 4 classifier misses |

- **ASR miss (1):** `en_scam_otp__far`: "Sir, I am call you just received." Every config dropped the middle words of this far-field recording. The built-in voice-activity filter may be cutting quiet speech. Try `vad_filter=False` (or `vad_parameters={"threshold": 0.3}`) for that clip.
- **Classifier misses (4):** all four Hinglish and Hindi OTP clips. The transcripts are **correct** ("Sir, aapka account block ho jaega. Abhi apna OTP bataie?") yet `otp_pii` scores only 0.03-0.05, and `urgency` only 0.02. Two causes:
  1. **Thin coverage:** your training data has only about 8 Hinglish OTP-request rows, and your gold set is English. The 0.98 F1 for `otp_pii` was measured on English.
  2. **Spelling variants:** with the exact spelling the model was trained on ("jayega … bataiye"), an earlier run scored 0.31 on the same sentence, versus 0.03 for "jaega … bataie".

## Fix: more Hinglish and Hindi OTP rows, plus spelling variants

**1. Batch 5** (36 rows: 30 OTP, PIN, CVV, password, Aadhaar and PAN requests in varied wording, plus 6 hard negatives). I checked it: no duplicates, no overlap with batches 2-4 or the probe. Save as `nlp/data/synthetic/batch5_otp_variants.csv`:

```csv
"आपका ओटीपी केवल आपके लिए है, इसे किसी को न बताएँ।",hindi,0,0,0,0,synthetic
"बैंक कभी फोन पर आपका पिन या सीवीवी नहीं पूछता।",hindi,0,0,0,0,synthetic
"Aapka transaction successful raha, koi OTP dene ki zarurat nahi hai.",hinglish,0,0,0,0,synthetic
```

**2. Spelling-variant script.** Save as `nlp/augment_variants.py` and run from `rakshavoice/`. It makes spelling variants of the romanized Hinglish rows (jaega/jayega, bataie/bataiye, rupe/rupaye) and ASR-style versions with no punctuation. I tested it: 84 Hinglish source rows give 150 new variants. The spelling map is generic Hinglish orthography, not copied from your test transcripts.

```python
import re, random, glob, pandas as pd
random.seed(0)
L = ["urgency", "authority", "otp_pii", "payment"]
VAR = {
 r"\bjayega\b": ["jaega", "jaayega", "jayegaa"],   r"\bjayegi\b": ["jaegi", "jaayegi"],
 r"\bbataiye\b": ["bataie", "batayiye", "bataiyae", "bataiye"],  r"\bbatao\b": ["bata do", "bataao"],
 r"\bdijiye\b": ["dijie", "dijiyega", "deejiye"], r"\bkijiye\b": ["kijie", "keejiye", "kijiyega"],
 r"\bbhejiye\b": ["bhejie", "bhejiyega"],
 r"\brupaye\b": ["rupe", "rupay", "rupiye", "rs"], r"\bpaanch\b": ["panch", "paach", "paanch"],
 r"\bhazaar\b": ["hazar", "hajar", "hajaar"],  r"\baapka\b": ["apka", "aapka", "ap ka"], r"\baapki\b": ["apki", "aapki"],
 r"\baapke\b": ["apke", "aapke"],  r"\bhoon\b": ["hun", "hu", "hoon"], r"\bmein\b": ["me", "mai", "mein"],
 r"\bwarna\b": ["varna", "warna", "nahi to"], r"\bjaldi\b": ["jldi", "jaldi", "jaldee"],
 r"\bkhaata\b": ["khata", "khaata"], r"\babhi\b": ["abhi", "abhee", "abi"],
}
def variant(t, p=0.7):
    out = t
    for pat, alts in VAR.items():
        if re.search(pat, out, re.I) and random.random() < p:
            out = re.sub(pat, random.choice(alts), out, flags=re.I)
    return out
def asr_style(t):
    t = re.sub(r"[.,?!]", "", t)
    return random.choice([t.lower(), t.lower(), t])

files = [f for f in glob.glob("nlp/data/synthetic/batch*_*.csv") if "reviewed" not in f]
df = pd.concat([pd.read_csv(f) for f in files], ignore_index=True)
df = df[df.lang == "hinglish"]
rows = []
for _, r in df.iterrows():
    for k in range(2):
        v = asr_style(variant(r.text))
        if v != r.text: rows.append({**r.to_dict(), "text": v, "source": "synthetic_variant"})
out = pd.DataFrame(rows).drop_duplicates("text")
out = out[~out.text.isin(df.text)]
out.to_csv("nlp/data/synthetic/variants.csv", index=False)
print("hinglish source rows:", len(df), "-> variants:", len(out), out[L].sum().to_dict())
```
(This version reads every `batch*_*.csv` in the folder except any named "reviewed". Check that your folder holds only your batch 1-5 files with that pattern, or tell me.)

**3. Patch `combine_training_data.py`:** add `"batch5_otp_variants.csv"` to the `SYN` list, and change the final concat so the variants are added once (not repeated four times):
```python
var_path = "nlp/data/synthetic/variants.csv"
var = pd.read_csv(var_path) if os.path.exists(var_path) else syn.iloc[0:0]
var["source"] = "synthetic_variant"
train = pd.concat([pos, neg] + [syn] * SYN_REPEAT + [var], ignore_index=True).sample(frac=1, random_state=0)
```

| Gold `otp_pii` (thr 0.4) | P 1.00, R 0.97, F1 0.98 | P 0.91, R 1.00, F1 0.95 (CI 0.89-1.0) |
| Gold `urgency` (thr 0.5) | P 0.82, R 0.64, F1 0.72 | P 0.77, R 0.71, F1 **0.74** (CI 0.62-0.84) |
| Probe `urgency` | P 0.52, R 0.81, F1 0.63 | P 0.72, R 1.00, F1 **0.84** (PR-AUC 0.97) |
| Probe `authority` | P 0.95, R 0.88, F1 0.91 | P 0.87, R 0.83, F1 0.85 |
| Probe `payment` | P 0.62, R 0.71, F1 0.67 | P 0.50, R 0.86, F1 0.63 (PR-AUC 0.54) |
| End-to-end clips (Hub84) | 31/36 | 31/36 |

`otp_pii` on gold is statistically the same (the confidence intervals overlap, the plateau from 0.30 to 0.65 is flat). `urgency` improved a lot on the probe. I can't say for certain why, since none of my changes targeted it. `authority` dipped slightly, and `payment` got worse.

### My mistake: the spelling variants made `payment` worse

Your `payment` false positives went from about 9 to **18**, almost all on authority threats (UIDAI, GST, immigration, PF office, Interpol, crime branch) in all three languages, at scores of 0.68-0.95. My `augment_variants.py` copied the batch-3 authority rows, about a third of which also carry `payment=1`, so it **strengthened the link** between "official threat" and "payment" instead of weakening it. I should have checked the label co-occurrence before augmenting.

The `GST notice by post, please consult your chartered accountant` negatives also fire `authority` (0.70-0.90): the model keys on "department" and "notice" words.

### Fix: a rule gate for `payment` (cheap and defensible)

A payment request needs a money cue in the text (rupees, fee, transfer, pay, deposit, UPI, amount, hazaar, and so on). The gate lowers the `payment` score to at most 0.10 when no such word appears. What I measured with it:

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Probe `payment` positives that contain a money cue | **21 of 21** |
| The 18 reported false-positive rows that contain one | **0 of 18** |
| Synthetic `payment` positives (batches 2-5) that contain one | 39 of 39 |
| Real audio: your English "cyber crime police" clips fired `payment`; the transcript has no money word | Gate removes it |
| Simulated probe `payment` at thr 0.5 | P 0.50 → **1.00**, R 0.86, F1 0.63 → **0.92** |

**Be honest about that last row:** I built the cue list after seeing the probe results, so the F1 of 0.92 is optimistic and the probe is now a development set. The rule is made of ordinary money words and it also passes the independent synthetic rows. It is a transparent hybrid design (learned model plus a lexical rule), which is easy to explain in a viva. For a clean final claim, test it later on fresh lines.

**Apply it in `nlp/intent_stream.py`.** Add this near the top (after the imports):
```python
import re
_MONEY_CUE = re.compile(r"rupee|rupaye|rupe\b|rupiye|\brs\b|₹|रुपये|रुपए|रुपया|paise|paisa|पैसे|पैसा|\bfees?\b|फीस|शुल्क|\bfine\b|jurmana|जुर्माना|penalty|deposit|\bjama\b|जमा|transfer|ट्रांसफर|\bpay\b|payment|paying|भुगतान|\bbhar\b|bharni|bharna|bhariye|भरना|भर दी|भर दो|भर लो|\bsend money\b|\bupi\b|यूपीआई|\bqr\b|क्यूआर|charges?\b|\bamount\b|राशि|challan|चालान|\bbail\b|जमानत|\brefund\b|रिफंड|\btax\b|savings|safe account|सुरक्षित खाते|surakshit khate|bachat|बचत|\bmoney\b|thousand|hundred|lakh|\bhaz(?:aa)?r\b|\bhajar\b|हज़ार|हजार|लाख|\bsau\b", re.I)
```
and in `score_text`, replace the line `p = _probs([text])[0]` with:
```python
    p = _probs([text])[0].copy()
    if "payment" in LABELS and not _MONEY_CUE.search(text):          # rule gate: a payment request needs an explicit money cue
        p[LABELS.index("payment")] = min(float(p[LABELS.index("payment")]), 0.10)
```
I syntax-checked it, but it isn't run on your machine. Test with: `python -c "from nlp.intent_stream import score_text; print(score_text('This is the cyber crime police. Your Aadhaar is linked to a case and you will be arrested today.'))"`. `payment` should now be 0.1 or lower, with `authority` still firing.

## Decisions

- **Model:** keep the **new** model. Averaged over the four labels' best evidence, it is about equal or slightly better (0.82 vs 0.80), and it has the added Hinglish OTP coverage.
- **Thresholds:** unchanged, `[0.50, 0.50, 0.40, 0.50]` (urgency, authority, otp_pii, payment). The `otp_pii` plateau is flat from 0.30 to 0.65.
- **ASR:** `MODEL = "Hub84/faster-whisper-hinglish-prime"`, `USE_PROMPT = False`.
- **Phase 3 status:** freeze it. The remaining weaknesses are documented limitations.

## Final limitations paragraph for the report
- `urgency` and `payment` labels are fuzzy by nature; `payment` is hybrid (a learned score plus a money-word rule).
- `authority` was evaluated on a 60-row synthetic probe, because real-data positives were too few; it misses some regulators (SEBI, UIDAI) and over-fires on "department notice" wording.
- Gold labels are AI-assisted and partly human-checked (110 rows reviewed, 4 changed), and the gold comes from the same source as the training data.
- End to end, Hindi and Hinglish detection depends on ASR quality, and a Hinglish-tuned Whisper raised the clips detected from 26/36 to 31/36.

## The most important finding: your laptop is still running the old model

```python
import librosa

y, _ = librosa.load("nlp/test_clips/en_scam_otp__far.wav", sr=16000)
```

## One last ASR setting: make the voice-activity filter more sensitive

Turning the filter off recovers the quiet clip, but off is risky in the live system: with no filter, Whisper tends to invent text ("Thank you.", repeated phrases) on silence and background hiss. A more sensitive filter should keep quiet speech without that risk. Test it (about 5 minutes), then decide:

Save this as `nlp/vad_test.py` and run it from `rakshavoice/`:
```python
import numpy as np, librosa
from nlp import asr

rng = np.random.RandomState(0)
tests = {
    "silence":  np.zeros(64000, dtype=np.float32),
    "hiss":     (rng.randn(64000) * 0.01).astype(np.float32),
    "far clip": librosa.load("nlp/test_clips/en_scam_otp__far.wav", sr=16000)[0],
}
settings = {"default (0.5)": dict(threshold=0.5), "0.3": dict(threshold=0.3), "0.2": dict(threshold=0.2), "filter off": None}
for sname, vp in settings.items():
    for tname, y in tests.items():
        kw = dict(vad_filter=False) if vp is None else dict(vad_filter=True, vad_parameters=vp)
        segs, _ = asr._m.transcribe(y, beam_size=1, temperature=0.0, condition_on_previous_text=False, **kw)
        print(f"{sname:14s} {tname:9s} | {' '.join(s.text.strip() for s in segs)[:90]!r}")
```
**Pick the most sensitive setting where `silence` and `hiss` still print an empty string** and the far clip recovers the full sentence. I expect `0.3` to work, but I haven't run it. (I only syntax-checked the script.)

Then make `nlp/asr.py` look like this (change the `VAD` line to your chosen threshold if it isn't 0.3):
```python
import numpy as np
from faster_whisper import WhisperModel

MODEL = "Hub84/faster-whisper-hinglish-prime"
try:
    _m = WhisperModel(MODEL, device="cuda", compute_type="int8_float16")
except Exception:
    _m = WhisperModel(MODEL, device="cpu", compute_type="int8")

VAD = dict(threshold=0.3, min_silence_duration_ms=500)      # more sensitive than the default 0.5, so quiet far-field speech is kept


def _run(wav, language):
    segs, info = _m.transcribe(wav, language=language, beam_size=1, vad_filter=True, vad_parameters=VAD,
                               temperature=0.0, condition_on_previous_text=False)
    return " ".join(s.text.strip() for s in segs).strip(), info.language


def transcribe(wav, language=None):
    """wav: float32 numpy array, 16 kHz mono."""
    wav = np.asarray(wav, dtype=np.float32)
    text, lang = _run(wav, language)
    if language is None and lang not in ("en", "hi"):          # guard against odd language guesses (e.g. Urdu script)
        text, _ = _run(wav, "hi")
    return text
```
Rerun `test_end_to_end.py` once more. If it says 36/36 and all 18 normal clips stay silent, you're done.

## Your VAD results

| Setting | Silence | Hiss | Far-field clip |
|---|---|---|---|
| default (0.5) | empty | empty | "Sir, I am calling you just received." (words lost) |
| **0.3** | empty | empty | full sentence, but "OTP" heard as **"ODP"** |
| 0.2 | empty | empty | same as 0.3 |
| filter off | empty | **"nan"** (invented text) | full sentence, "OTP" correct |

Two things come out of this:
1. **Filter off is unsafe:** Whisper invented the text "nan" from background hiss, which is exactly what the filter prevents. Keep the filter on.
2. **0.3 is the setting to use.** It is the least aggressive setting that recovers the quiet speech, and 0.2 gave identical results on these tests, so there is no reason to take the extra risk of passing more background noise to Whisper in real calls.

Remaining problem: with the filter on, the far-field clip is heard as "ODP" instead of "OTP". I added a small repair step for that, below.

## The new `nlp/asr.py` (replace the whole file)

```python
import re
import numpy as np
from faster_whisper import WhisperModel

MODEL = "Hub84/faster-whisper-hinglish-prime"
try:
    _m = WhisperModel(MODEL, device="cuda", compute_type="int8_float16")
except Exception:
    _m = WhisperModel(MODEL, device="cpu", compute_type="int8")

# Chosen with vad_test.py: 0.3 keeps quiet far-field speech, and silence / hiss still give empty text.
# (With the filter fully off, Whisper invented the text 'nan' on hiss.)
VAD = dict(threshold=0.3, min_silence_duration_ms=500)

# Small repair list for key terms Whisper sometimes mishears or spells out. Extend it as you find new cases.
_FIXES = [
    (re.compile(r"\bO\.?\s?T\.?\s?P\.?\b", re.I), "OTP"),      # "O T P", "o.t.p."
    (re.compile(r"\bO[DB]P\b", re.I), "OTP"),                  # "ODP", "OBP" (heard once on a far-field clip)
    (re.compile(r"\bU\.?\s?P\.?\s?I\.?\b", re.I), "UPI"),
    (re.compile(r"\bC\.?\s?V\.?\s?V\.?\b", re.I), "CVV"),
    (re.compile(r"\bK\.?\s?Y\.?\s?C\.?\b", re.I), "KYC"),
    (re.compile(r"\bC\.?\s?B\.?\s?I\.?\b", re.I), "CBI"),
]


def _repair(text):
    for pat, rep in _FIXES:
        text = pat.sub(rep, text)
    return text


def _run(wav, language):
    segs, info = _m.transcribe(wav, language=language, beam_size=1, vad_filter=True, vad_parameters=VAD,
                               temperature=0.0, condition_on_previous_text=False)
    return " ".join(s.text.strip() for s in segs).strip(), info.language


def transcribe(wav, language=None):
    """wav: float32 numpy array, 16 kHz mono. Returns the transcript text."""
    wav = np.asarray(wav, dtype=np.float32)
    text, lang = _run(wav, language)
    if language is None and lang not in ("en", "hi"):          # guard against odd language guesses (e.g. Urdu script)
        text, _ = _run(wav, "hi")
    return _repair(text)
```

I syntax-checked it and tested the repair patterns on sample sentences. They fix "ODP", "O T P", "U P I", "C V V" and leave normal text alone ("open to the public", "odd", "pin" and "CBI" are untouched), but the file has not been run against Whisper on your machine.

What each part does:
- **`VAD`**: the more sensitive voice-activity filter (threshold 0.3).
- **`_FIXES`**: the repair list. Only the "ODP" case was actually observed, in one far-field clip. The other patterns (spelled-out letters like "O T P" or "U P I") are common Whisper behaviour that I added as precaution, so treat the list as a starting set.
- **`transcribe()`**: same behaviour as before, plus the Hindi re-run when Whisper guesses an odd language, and the repair at the end.

## Phase 4: the voiceprint stream

**Goal:** a function `score_voice(wav, "contact_name")` that says how likely the caller is **not** the enrolled person, in the same message format as the other streams.

**Where it runs:** your laptop. The ECAPA model is small (about 90 MB), so it adds very little GPU memory. Nothing in this phase needs Kaggle.

### Design decisions

1. **When it is active.** The voice stream only makes sense when the caller *claims* to be someone you enrolled (for example "your son"). In the prototype, the user picks that contact from a dropdown before or during the call. With nobody selected, the stream stays neutral and fusion ignores it.
2. **What is stored.** Only an averaged **embedding** (192 numbers) per enrolled person, plus a small consent record. No audio.
3. **Fix a contradiction in your synopsis.** The legal table says embeddings are "held only for the duration of the call". But enrolment requires saving the enrolled person's embedding. Reword it as: *"Caller-side audio and embeddings are discarded after each chunk. Enrolled voiceprints are stored only on the user's device, with that person's separate consent, and can be deleted at any time."* That is accurate and still defensible under the DPDP Act.
4. **Model:** the pretrained SpeechBrain ECAPA-TDNN (`speechbrain/spkrec-ecapa-voxceleb`), trained on VoxCeleb1 and 2 and scoring with cosine similarity, as your synopsis specifies (frozen feature extractor, and the calibration on top is yours). It is Apache-2.0 licensed and expects 16 kHz mono audio.

### Phase 4 steps

| Step | What | Needs people? | Time |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Install SpeechBrain and test one embedding | No | 20 min |
| 2 | Add `voice/voice_stream.py` | No | 20 min |
| 3 | Evaluate on public data (LibriSpeech), including a phone-like condition, and calibrate | No | 2 hours |
| 4 | Record and enrol real speakers (with consent) | **Yes** | 1 day |
| 5 | Evaluate on your recordings: clean, far-field, speakerphone, Hindi and English | **Yes** | 1 day |
| 6 | Wire the calibrated score into the shared stream format; log results | No | 1 hour |

I'll detail Steps 1-3 now, since they don't need other people.

### Step 1: Install and test


### Step 2: `voice/voice_stream.py`
Create the folders `voice/enrolled/` and `voice/results/`, add an empty `voice/__init__.py`, and save this. I syntax-checked it but couldn't run it here (no SpeechBrain or audio), so expect to fix a small error on first run.

```python
import json, datetime
from pathlib import Path
import numpy as np, torch
from speechbrain.inference.speaker import EncoderClassifier

ROOT = Path(__file__).parent
ENROLLED = ROOT / "enrolled"
DEV = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"

_clf = EncoderClassifier.from_hparams(source="speechbrain/spkrec-ecapa-voxceleb",
                                      savedir=str(ROOT / "pretrained_ecapa"), run_opts={"device": DEV})


def embed(wav):
    """wav: float32 numpy, 16 kHz mono -> L2-normalised 192-d embedding (numpy)."""
    x = torch.from_numpy(np.asarray(wav, dtype=np.float32)).unsqueeze(0).to(DEV)
    with torch.no_grad():
        e = _clf.encode_batch(x).squeeze()
    return torch.nn.functional.normalize(e, dim=0).cpu().numpy()


def similarity(a, b):
    return float(np.dot(a, b))                                   # both L2-normalised, so this is cosine similarity


def enroll(name, wavs, consent_text):
    """Store ONLY the averaged embedding (no audio). consent_text records what the enrolled person agreed to."""
    if not consent_text.strip():
        raise ValueError("Enrolment needs the enrolled person's consent text.")
    embs = np.stack([embed(w) for w in wavs])
    c = embs.mean(0); c /= np.linalg.norm(c)
    ENROLLED.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
    np.save(ENROLLED / f"{name}.npy", c)
    json.dump({"name": name, "n_clips": len(wavs), "consent": consent_text,
               "date": datetime.date.today().isoformat()}, open(ENROLLED / f"{name}.json", "w"), indent=2)


def delete_enrolled(name):
    for ext in (".npy", ".json"):
        (ENROLLED / f"{name}{ext}").unlink(missing_ok=True)


def list_enrolled():
    return sorted(p.stem for p in ENROLLED.glob("*.npy")) if ENROLLED.exists() else []


def _calibration():
    p = ROOT / "calibration.json"
    return json.load(open(p)) if p.exists() else {"a": 12.0, "c": -6.0, "threshold": 0.5}   # placeholder until Step 3 runs


def score_voice(wav, name):
    """Shared stream format. score = probability the caller is NOT the enrolled person (0 = same, 1 = different)."""
    cal = _calibration()
    sim = similarity(embed(wav), np.load(ENROLLED / f"{name}.npy"))
    p_same = 1.0 / (1.0 + np.exp(-(cal["a"] * sim + cal["c"])))
    return {"stream": "voice", "score": round(float(1.0 - p_same), 4), "similarity": round(sim, 4),
            "enrolled": name, "evidence": ["voice_mismatch"] if sim < cal["threshold"] else []}
```
`enroll()` refuses to run without a consent text, so the consent step from your synopsis is built into the code.

**Quick test:** run this from `rakshavoice/` with two of your own recordings (use a clip from `nlp/test_clips/` as one, and a second recording of your voice):
```python
import librosa
from voice.voice_stream import embed, similarity
a = embed(librosa.load("nlp/test_clips/en_norm_emi__clean.wav", sr=16000)[0])
b = embed(librosa.load("nlp/test_clips/hg_norm_emi__clean.wav", sr=16000)[0])
print(a.shape, similarity(a, b))
```
Both clips are your voice, so expect a shape of `(192,)` and a similarity clearly above 0.5 (typically about 0.6-0.9). Send me the number.

### Step 3: Public-data evaluation (2 hours)
This gives you an EER and a calibration without needing anyone else, and shows how much accuracy drops for short chunks and phone-quality audio.

1. Download `test-clean.tar.gz` from openslr.org/12 (about 350 MB) and extract it to `voice/data/` so that `voice/data/LibriSpeech/test-clean/<speaker>/<chapter>/*.flac` exists. Add `voice/data/` to `.gitignore`.
2. Save this as `voice/evaluate_public.py` and run `python -m voice.evaluate_public` from `rakshavoice/`:

```python
import glob, os, json, random
import numpy as np, soundfile as sf, librosa
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.metrics import roc_curve, roc_auc_score
from voice.voice_stream import embed

ROOT = "voice/data/LibriSpeech/test-clean"
SR, N_UTT, N_ENROL = 16000, 12, 3
random.seed(0); rng = np.random.RandomState(0)

def load_files():
    out = {}
    for d in sorted(glob.glob(f"{ROOT}/*")):
        files = sorted(glob.glob(f"{d}/*/*.flac")); random.shuffle(files)
        if len(files) >= N_UTT: out[os.path.basename(d)] = files[:N_UTT]
    return out

def read(f):
    y, sr = sf.read(f, dtype="float32")
    return y if sr == SR else librosa.resample(y, orig_sr=sr, target_sr=SR)

def crop(y, sec):
    n = int(sec * SR)
    if len(y) < n: y = np.pad(y, (0, n - len(y)))
    s = rng.randint(0, len(y) - n + 1)
    return y[s:s + n]

def phone(y, snr_db=20):                                   # narrow-band (8 kHz) + noise, a rough stand-in for a phone call
    y = librosa.resample(librosa.resample(y, orig_sr=SR, target_sr=8000), orig_sr=8000, target_sr=SR)
    return (y + rng.randn(len(y)) * np.std(y) / (10 ** (snr_db / 20))).astype(np.float32)

def eer(y, s):
    fpr, tpr, _ = roc_curve(y, s); fnr = 1 - tpr
    i = np.nanargmin(np.abs(fnr - fpr)); return float((fpr[i] + fnr[i]) / 2)

files = load_files(); spk = list(files)
audio = {s: [read(f) for f in files[s]] for s in spk}
print(len(spk), "speakers")

def run(sec, phone_test):
    cent = {}
    for s in spk:                                          # enrol from clean 8 s crops
        e = np.stack([embed(crop(y, 8)) for y in audio[s][:N_ENROL]]); c = e.mean(0); cent[s] = c / np.linalg.norm(c)
    scores, labels = [], []
    for s in spk:
        for y in audio[s][N_ENROL:]:
            x = crop(y, sec); x = phone(x) if phone_test else x
            e = embed(x)
            for t in spk:
                scores.append(float(np.dot(e, cent[t]))); labels.append(int(t == s))
    return np.array(labels), np.array(scores)

results, keep = {}, None
for name, sec, ph in [("clean_2s", 2, False), ("clean_4s", 4, False), ("phone_2s", 2, True), ("phone_4s", 4, True)]:
    y, s = run(sec, ph)
    results[name] = {"EER": eer(y, s), "AUC": float(roc_auc_score(y, s)), "target_trials": int(y.sum()), "nontarget_trials": int((1 - y).sum())}
    print(f"{name:9s} EER={results[name]['EER']*100:5.2f}%  AUC={results[name]['AUC']:.4f}")
    if name == "phone_4s": keep = (y, s)

y, s = keep                                                 # calibrate on the most realistic condition
lr = LogisticRegression(class_weight="balanced").fit(s.reshape(-1, 1), y)
a, c = float(lr.coef_[0][0]), float(lr.intercept_[0])
fpr, tpr, thr = roc_curve(y, s); i = np.nanargmin(np.abs((1 - tpr) - fpr))
cal = {"a": a, "c": c, "threshold": float(thr[i])}
os.makedirs("voice/results", exist_ok=True)
json.dump(results, open("voice/results/public_eval.json", "w"), indent=2)
json.dump(cal, open("voice/calibration.json", "w"), indent=2)
print("calibration:", cal)
```

**What it measures:** 40 speakers; each is enrolled from 3 clean 8-second clips and tested on 9 further short chunks (2 s and 4 s), each scored against *every* enrolled speaker. That gives target trials (same speaker) and non-target trials (different speaker). It repeats this with a "phone-like" version of the test audio (8 kHz narrow-band plus noise), which is the realistic case because a speakerphone call is narrow-band and enrolment is clean. It then saves the EER and a calibration (`voice/calibration.json`) so the score becomes a probability.

**What to expect (rough, not guaranteed):** clean 4 s EER of a few percent or lower, noticeably worse for 2 s chunks and for phone-like audio. LibriSpeech is clean English read speech, so it is the *easy* case. Real Indian-accented and speakerphone audio will be harder, which is why Step 5 matters.

## Your results

| Check | Result | Reading |
|---|---|---|
| Two clips of your own voice | Similarity **0.82**, shape `(192,)` | Works. Well above the threshold (about 0.30) |
| Clean 2 s / 4 s chunks | EER 0.45% / 0.87% | Very good |
| Phone-like 2 s / 4 s chunks | EER 1.69% / 1.69% | Small drop |
| Calibration | `a = 23.6, c = -7.32`, threshold **0.298** | A similarity of 0.82 maps to a mismatch score of about 0 |

Please don't read these too finely. Each condition has only 360 same-speaker trials, so one trial is about 0.28% EER. That is why 2 s looks better than 4 s: the difference is within noise, so the honest summary is "roughly 0.5-1.7% on all four." Also remember what the test is: 40 clean English speakers reading books, and the "phone" version is only a crude simulation (8 kHz plus noise), not a real phone codec. Real Indian-accented, speakerphone audio will be harder, and relatives who sound alike are harder still. An attacker using a cloned voice is a separate test, in Phase 8.

## Your question: will Phases 3 and 4 change the backend and frontend?

**Yes, eventually, but not yet.** Right now:
- `backend/main.py` only calls `score_chunk` (the spoof detector), and the React page only shows the spoof gauge. Phases 3 and 4 built the *stream functions* (`score_text`, `transcribe`, `score_voice`) in `nlp/` and `voice/`, and nothing calls them yet. That is normal, and it matches my plan, which put the full integration in Phase 7.

**But I'd move a small slice of it earlier**, right after Phase 4 (about one day), because it removes risk:
- It tests the real pipeline's latency and GPU memory with all three models running together, instead of finding problems in Week 8.
- The fusion network (Phase 5) needs **real logged score sequences** from live test calls, and a unified message format to train on.
- The windowing decisions (4 s windows, 2 s hop, overlapping transcripts) only show up in live runs.

### What would change

**Backend (`backend/main.py`):**
1. For each 2 s hop, run the spoof model, `transcribe` then `score_text`, and, if a contact is selected, `score_voice`.
2. Accept two message types on the WebSocket: binary audio (as now) and small JSON control messages such as `{"type": "select_contact", "name": "mom"}`. At the moment the server only calls `receive_bytes()`.
3. Send one combined message back (schema below).
4. Don't log transcripts or audio. Your synopsis promises only the numeric verdict and evidence trail are kept. A transcript is personal data, so show it in the page and discard it.

**Frontend (`App.jsx`), small changes:**
1. Show the latest transcript line.
2. Show four label chips (urgency, authority, otp_pii, payment) with their scores.
3. A dropdown, "Caller claims to be: none / contact names", and a voice-match indicator.
4. Keep the spoof gauge. Later, a fused risk gauge, the explanation sentence and the challenge question (Phases 5 and 6).

For the prototype, **enrolment can be a command-line script** (record 3 clips, confirm a consent prompt, store the embedding). That keeps the UI small.

### One message format for everything (so later phases plug in)
```json
{
  "t": 1760000000.1,
  "window_s": 4,
  "transcript": "Sir, I am calling from your bank...",
  "streams": {
    "spoof":  {"score": 0.12},
    "intent": {"score": 0.97, "labels": {"urgency": 0.06, "authority": 0.02, "otp_pii": 0.98, "payment": 0.1}, "evidence": ["otp_pii"]},
    "voice":  {"score": 0.03, "similarity": 0.81, "enrolled": "mom", "evidence": []}
  }
}
```
(`voice` is only present when a contact is selected.)

### Latency estimate
About 0.2 s (spoof) + 0.6 s (Whisper) + 0.03 s (intent) + about 0.05 s (voice) is under 1 s per 2 s hop, so running them one after another is fine. I'll write the integration code when we get there, after Phase 4 is finished.

## Next in Phase 4: a real test with your own voice, today

You have 18 clean and 18 far-field clips of **your** voice in Indian-accented English, Hinglish and Hindi. That is a real accent and a real far-field condition, and it needs no other people. The script enrols you from your clean *normal-call* clips, tests on your *scam-line* clips (clean and far), and uses the 40 LibriSpeech speakers as impostors. I syntax-checked it, but couldn't run it here.

Save as `voice/own_voice_test.py` and run `python -m voice.own_voice_test` from `rakshavoice/`:

```python
import glob, json, os, random
import numpy as np, librosa, soundfile as sf
from sklearn.metrics import roc_curve
from voice.voice_stream import embed

SR = 16000
rng = np.random.RandomState(0); random.seed(0)
cal = json.load(open("voice/calibration.json")); THR = cal["threshold"]

def windows(y, sec=4, hop=2):
    n = int(sec * SR)
    return [y[i:i + n] for i in range(0, max(1, len(y) - n + 1), int(hop * SR)) if np.sqrt(np.mean(y[i:i + n] ** 2)) > 0.005]

def clip_embs(path):
    y = librosa.load(path, sr=SR, mono=True)[0]
    return [embed(w) for w in windows(y)]

enrol_files = sorted(glob.glob("nlp/test_clips/*_norm_*__clean.wav"))
target = {"clean": sorted(glob.glob("nlp/test_clips/*_scam_*__clean.wav")),
          "far":   sorted(glob.glob("nlp/test_clips/*_scam_*__far.wav"))}
E = [e for f in enrol_files for e in clip_embs(f)]
c = np.mean(E, axis=0); c /= np.linalg.norm(c)
print("enrolment windows:", len(E), "from", len(enrol_files), "clips")

scores, labels = [], []
for cond, files in target.items():
    s = [float(np.dot(e, c)) for f in files for e in clip_embs(f)]
    print(f"{cond:6s} target windows={len(s):3d}  mean similarity={np.mean(s):.3f}  min={np.min(s):.3f}  below threshold={np.mean(np.array(s) < THR)*100:.1f}%")
    scores += s; labels += [1] * len(s)

imp = []
for d in sorted(glob.glob("voice/data/LibriSpeech/test-clean/*")):
    files = glob.glob(f"{d}/*/*.flac")
    for f in random.sample(files, 3):
        y, sr = sf.read(f, dtype="float32"); y = y if sr == SR else librosa.resample(y, orig_sr=sr, target_sr=SR)
        s0 = rng.randint(0, max(1, len(y) - 4 * SR)); imp.append(float(np.dot(embed(y[s0:s0 + 4 * SR]), c)))
print(f"impostor trials={len(imp)}  mean similarity={np.mean(imp):.3f}  max={np.max(imp):.3f}  above threshold={np.mean(np.array(imp) >= THR)*100:.1f}%")
scores += imp; labels += [0] * len(imp)

y, s = np.array(labels), np.array(scores)
fpr, tpr, _ = roc_curve(y, s); i = np.nanargmin(np.abs((1 - tpr) - fpr))
print(f"EER (you vs 40 LibriSpeech speakers) = {(fpr[i] + (1 - tpr[i])) / 2 * 100:.2f}%   threshold used = {THR:.3f}")
```

**How to read it:** if the "far" clips' mean similarity is much lower than "clean", and a noticeable share fall below the threshold, then speakerphone-quality audio hurts, and I'll adjust the calibration. Limitation to state in the report: this is **one real speaker**, so it checks accent and far-field behaviour, not a full evaluation.
